    /// Verified: the enumerated form fails at 7.20 with `{4,5,6,7}` and passes
    /// at 6.90. Raising 48 to 480 shrinks the probability instead of removing
    /// it and is not a fix.
    ///
    /// Assert the attained **set**, not containment — a refit collapsing every
    /// universe to 5 satisfies `.all(|v| (4..=6).contains(v))`.
    #[test]
    fn valence_ceiling_is_four_to_six_over_the_drawn_range() {
        let mut seen = std::collections::BTreeSet::new();
        for k in 6..=14 {
            for n in 60..=120 {
                seen.insert(valence_ceiling_at(k, n));
            }
        }
        let expected: std::collections::BTreeSet<u8> = [4, 5, 6].into_iter().collect();
        assert_eq!(
            seen, expected,
            "the attained valence ceiling set moved over the full drawn grid"
        );
    }

    /// §7.1: mass increases down the table. The fitted series this replaced
    /// stopped increasing at N = 124 — so the third closed shell weighed less
    /// than the element below it — and a 120-element table did not show it.
    #[test]
    fn mass_increases_with_index() {
        for seed in 0..12 {
            let (_, els) = table(seed);
            for w in els.windows(2) {
                assert!(w[1].mass > w[0].mass, "seed {seed}: {} !> {}", w[1].name, w[0].name);
            }
        }
    }

    /// **`Mass::from_raw` validates nothing** — its own doc says so, and `impl
    /// Add` wraps in release on an out-of-range value. Since the construction is
    /// now integer, grid-exactness is a property of the type rather than a claim
    /// about the series; what still needs checking is that the drawn range
    /// cannot leave `MAX_MAGNITUDE`.
    ///
    /// Three drafts of this test were wrong in three ways. The first compared
    /// `Mass` to `Result<Mass, _>` and did not compile. The second round-tripped
    /// an already-quantised value — `to_f64` is an exact division by a power of
    /// two and `from_f64_quantised` an exact multiplication back, so it could
    /// not fail for anything the construction can produce, verified over
    /// 2 000 000 values. The third *described* the repair in this comment and
    /// left the round-trip in the body, which is how it reached review.
    ///
    /// **Assert the raw sub-unit count against the tight bound**, and the
    /// reason is *tightness* — nothing to do with overflow.
    ///
    /// A fourth draft justified this by saying `from_raw` cannot catch
    /// `units·base_sub` overflowing `i64`, and that a round-trip through
    /// `MAX_MAGNITUDE` would miss the wrapped value. Both halves are wrong.
    /// The overflow needs `units >= 2^63/1792 ≈ 5.2e15` against a loop bound of
    /// 120 — unreachable by thirteen orders of magnitude. And
    /// `from_f64_quantised` rejects `|raw| > 2^30`, so of the `2^64` wrapped
    /// values it is blind to only about 1 in 8.6e9; the round-trip would have
    /// caught essentially every one. That is the fourth consecutive
    /// justification on this test to name the wrong mechanism, which is worth
    /// more than the test itself as a warning.
    ///
    /// What the tight bound actually buys: a draw range that moves fails
    /// *here*, instead of silently spending 4993x of headroom. `MAX_MAGNITUDE`
    /// would not notice.
    ///
    /// The bound is analytic and does not go stale when the draw ranges move:
    /// `units·base_sub ≤ 120 × 1792 = 215 040`, defect ≤ 5 196, against a
    /// representable 1 073 741 824 — 4 993× headroom. Assert the *tight* bound,
    /// so a change to a draw range fails here rather than quietly spending that
    /// headroom.
    #[test]
    fn every_mass_is_inside_the_range_from_raw_does_not_check() {
        // `const`, not `checked_mul(..).expect(..)`: const evaluation makes an
        // overflowing product a *compile* error (E0080), which is strictly
        // stronger than a runtime check, and avoids an `expect` that
        // `clippy::expect_used` denies — `clippy.toml` sets neither
        // `allow-expect-in-tests` nor `allow-unwrap-in-tests`, so the deny
        // reaches inside `#[cfg(test)]`. See Step 8b.
        const MAX_UNITS: i64 = 120;
        const MAX_BASE_SUB: i64 = 1792;
        const CEILING: i64 = MAX_UNITS * MAX_BASE_SUB;
        for seed in 0..24 {
            let (_, els) = table(seed);
            for e in els {
                let raw = e.mass.raw();
                assert!(
                    (1..=CEILING).contains(&raw),
                    "seed {seed} N={}: raw {raw} outside 1..={CEILING} — \
                     a draw range moved",
                    e.units
                );
            }
        }
    }

    /// A closed shell has no frontier, so it has no unmade lateral contacts.
    /// This is the mechanism's headline output and what §7.2's "the most
    /// tightly bound element cannot bond" reading rests on.
    ///
    /// **It was in the probe and not carried across, and nothing else covers
    /// it.** Measured: a mutation breaking both zeroing factors puts every
    /// closure at valence 1 and the whole suite still passes — continuity
    /// trivially (1→1), the ceiling test, and `elements_one_past_a_closure`.
    ///
    /// It is *not* the test that distinguishes mechanism from declaration; two
    /// independent factors hold the zero, so a declaration can be laid over a
    /// broken formula and this still passes. That claim is a property of the
    /// source — see the `xtask` check in Step 8c.
    #[test]
    fn closed_shells_have_valence_zero() {
        for seed in 0..24 {
            let (sp, els) = table(seed);
            for n in packing::closures(sp.k, els.len()) {
                if let Some(e) = els.get(n - 1) {
                    assert_eq!(e.valence, 0, "seed {seed}: N={n} is a closure");
                }
            }
        }
    }

    /// Affinity is `2 * exposed_fraction - 1`, so it cannot leave `(-1, 1]`
    /// unless burial is miscounted. The predecessor reached exactly +1.000 at
    /// every closure by reading the capacity of the *empty* shell, making the
    /// stable elements the stickiest and stopping anything accumulating.
    #[test]
    fn affinity_is_in_range_and_continuous() {
        for seed in 0..12 {
            let (_, els) = table(seed);
            assert!(els.iter().all(|e| e.affinity > -1.0 && e.affinity <= 1.0));
            for w in els.windows(2) {
                let bound = 2.0 / w[0].units as f64;
                assert!(
                    (w[1].affinity - w[0].affinity).abs() <= bound,
                    "seed {seed} N={}: affinity jumped {}",
                    w[0].units,
                    (w[1].affinity - w[0].affinity).abs()
                );
            }
        }
    }

    /// The binding peak sits at a closure by *mechanism* — a closed shell has
    /// no frontier, so it maximises contacts per unit and the energy series is
    /// a sawtooth. If a change smooths the series across closures this fails,
    /// which is the point: the predecessor's minimum was the distance from a
    /// typed-in constant and looked identical from outside.
    #[test]
    fn energy_per_unit_is_locally_maximal_at_every_closure() {
        let (shell, els) = table(3);
        for &c in shell.closures.iter().filter(|&&c| c > 1) {
            let (lo, at, hi) = (els.get(c - 2), els.get(c - 1), els.get(c));
            if let (Some(lo), Some(at), Some(hi)) = (lo, at, hi) {
                assert!(
                    at.energy_per_unit > lo.energy_per_unit
                        && at.energy_per_unit > hi.energy_per_unit,
                    "closure {c} is not a local energy maximum"
                );
            }
        }
    }

    /// `peak` is an output. If it took only one value across universes it
    /// would merely have moved from an argument to a hard-coded location.
    #[test]
    fn the_binding_peak_moves_with_the_seed() {
        let peaks: std::collections::BTreeSet<usize> =
            (0..24).map(|s| table(s).0.peak).collect();
        assert!(peaks.len() >= 6, "peak took only {} values: {peaks:?}", peaks.len());
    }

    /// Different universes must get different table *shapes*, not the same
    /// shape with different numbers in it.
    #[test]
    fn shell_patterns_vary_between_universes() {
        let patterns: std::collections::BTreeSet<Vec<usize>> =
            (0..30).map(|s| table(s).0.closures).collect();
        assert!(patterns.len() > 5, "shell patterns barely vary: {}", patterns.len());
    }

    #[test]
    fn table_size_is_workable() {
        for seed in 0..30 {
            let n = table(seed).1.len();
            // The draw is 60..=120, so the lower half of a `40..=120`
            // assertion was a guard that could not fire — the defect class this
            // task is partly about, asserted 200 lines from a comment rejecting
            // it.
            assert!((60..=120).contains(&n), "seed {seed} produced {n} elements");
        }
    }
}

- [x] **Step 11: Implement element generation**

Prepend to `crates/borbax-universe/src/element.rs`:

```rust
//! The generated periodic table (spec §7.1).
//!
//! **Element `N` is `N` copies of one base unit, packed.** Every property below
//! is a function of that packing rather than a curve chosen because it looked
//! plausible — which is principle 2 applied to the file that defines what an
//! atom is. See Task 4's preamble in the plan for what is claimed and, more
//! importantly, what is not: there is no shape-diversity advantage, only the
//! measured finding that a fusion-derived radius series costs nothing.
//!
//! Periodicity is a *consequence* here. A shell closes when it fills, and the
//! elements at and around a closure form families with a cause: a closed shell
//! has no frontier and so no bonding slots, and the element one unit past any
//! closure has exactly one.

use crate::naming;
use crate::packing::{self, PackingConsts};
use borbax_rng::{Domain, Stream};
use borbax_units::{Mass, Span, det_math};

/// Index into a universe's element table. `u8` because tables are capped
/// well below 256, which keeps `Mol12`'s atom array one byte per slot.
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, PartialOrd, Ord, Hash)]
pub struct ElementId(pub u8);

#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
pub struct Element {
    pub id: ElementId,
    pub symbol: String,
    pub name: String,
    /// Base units in this element's cluster. The element *is* this number.
    pub units: usize,
    /// Which shell is filling. The period index, derived rather than drawn.
    pub period: u8,
    /// Units in the incomplete outer shell. The group index.
    pub group: u8,
    pub mass: Mass,
    /// Bonding slots: unmade lateral contacts on the frontier, over the
    /// lateral coordination. Zero at a closure because a closed shell has no
    /// frontier — not because a branch says so.
    pub valence: u8,
    /// Exposed fraction of the cluster, mapped to [-1, +1]. Drives
    /// complementarity in §8.3. **Not** electronegativity: different range,
    /// different meaning, different behaviour (G3).
    pub affinity: f64,
    pub radius: Span,
    /// Binding energy per unit: made contacts, less radial strain.
    ///
    /// **Task 5 must derive `bond_energies` from this**, not draw an
    /// independent matrix. Two encodings of "these elements bind well" in
    /// incommensurable units is the defect that made the predecessor's `peak`
    /// unfalsifiable, one level up.
    ///
    /// **G4 note, flagged deliberately rather than silently accepted.** This is
    /// an energy travelling as a bare `f64` into a task that mints it as
    /// [`Quanta`] at the far end, with no type-level relationship — the exact
    /// "bare `f64` carrying a quantity through three functions defeats this
    /// quietly" shape §5's G4 exists to stop. `borbax-units` provides
    /// `Div<f64> for Quanta`, so cluster-energy-per-unit is expressible as
    /// `Quanta` and this *could* be typed. It is left bare for now because
    /// `energy_per_unit` is also compared, summed and scaled inside this file
    /// where the newtype buys nothing, and changing it is Task 5's call when it
    /// writes the consumer. **Task 5 must decide explicitly**, not inherit the
    /// `f64` by default — that decision is what sets the precedent for every
    /// energy that follows.
    pub energy_per_unit: f64,
    /// How far below the binding-energy peak this element sits, on `0..=1`.
    /// 0 at the peak, rising toward both extremes.
    ///
    /// **The shipped doc is longer and this is a pointer to it, not a copy.**
    /// See `element.rs` — it records why "decay" was the wrong word (two
    /// unrelated processes shared it), why the name deliberately does **not**
    /// say "rate" (§7.1 and Task 15 disagree about the type, so a name that
    /// picks a side would let a reader inherit an answer), and the one-axis
    /// limitation on what this can model.
    ///
    /// This block previously carried the pre-rename text with the new
    /// identifier spliced into it, which read as a fresh claim that
    /// `instability` is a probability per world-year — the exact claim the
    /// rename exists to withhold.
    pub instability: f64,
    /// Relative abundance. **The gel lever** (§7.2) — see `generate_elements`.
    pub abundance: f64,
    /// Which band of outer-shell occupancy this element exposes — `floor(fill ×
    /// n_bands)`.
    ///
    /// **Not derived, and this doc is what `cargo doc` renders.** An earlier
    /// version said "coordination class of the frontier sites"; no coordination
    /// mix is computed anywhere, and enumerated across all `k`, `n_bands`
    /// and `N` this takes exactly one value per `(outer, cap)` pair — zero
    /// information beyond `group`. Nothing downstream may branch on it as
    /// though it carried chemistry.
    pub outer_fill_band: u8,
}

/// The derived shape of one universe's table.
#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
pub struct ShellPattern {
    /// The drawn part of the shell law: shell `n` holds `k*n^2 + 2` units.
    pub k: usize,
    /// Cumulative unit counts at which a shell closes. Derived from `k`, not
    /// drawn — this replaces the predecessor's drawn table of period lengths.
    pub closures: Vec<usize>,
    /// Where the per-unit binding energy peaks. **Read off the finished
    /// series, never passed in** — that is the property distinguishing this
    /// from the predecessor, in which `peak` was an argument whose value was
    /// then reported back as an emergent minimum.
    pub peak: usize,
}

/// Convenience for tests and callers that need the universe-domain stream.
#[must_use]
pub fn stream(seed: u64) -> Stream {
    Stream::new(seed, Domain::Universe, 0)
}

/// Build a universe's periodic table by fusion.
///
/// **There is no `peak` parameter and there must never be one.** The
/// predecessor took one, computed instability as `((N - peak)/peak)^2`, and
/// reported the resulting minimum as emergent — it was the distance from a
/// typed-in constant, and sweeping `peak` landed the minimum on a closure in
/// 13 of 24 draws. Here one binding energy per unit is derived from the
/// packing counts and both the mass defect and the decay rate are functions
/// of it.
///
/// Infallible: the mass is built in sub-units and handed to `Mass::from_raw`,
/// so there is no range check to fail and no error to propagate. See the task
/// preamble for why a draft made it fallible and why that was wrong.
pub fn generate_elements(seed: u64) -> (ShellPattern, Vec<Element>) {
    let mut rng = stream(seed);

    // `k` spans 6..=14. Below 6 a shell cannot triangulate a sphere; above 14
    // the second shell exceeds 200 units, so a 120-element table never leaves
    // it and there are no families at all.
    let k = 6 + rng.next_range(9) as usize;
    // `z = k + 2` is forced by the shell law, not chosen: `shell(1) = k + 2` and
    // shell 1 *is* the units touching the core. An earlier `6 + k/2` agreed only
    // at k = 8 and put 16 sites in shell 1 around a core with 13 neighbours.
    // Construct through `PackingConsts::new` so the relation has one home.
    let pack = PackingConsts::new(k);

    // **`base_mass` must be dyadic and `contact_defect` need not be.** `Mass`
    // is fixed-point at SCALE = 1024; `units * base_mass` is never rounded, so
    // that factor has to land on the grid by itself, while the defect is
    // quantised explicitly below. Probed both ways: drawing `base_mass` in
    // steps of 0.3 fails exactness at N = 1, changing `contact_defect`'s
    // denominator from 512 to 500 changes nothing, and deleting the
    // quantisation fails at N = 2. An earlier comment called both
    // load-bearing, which would have sent a future reader to guard the wrong
    // half.
    let base_mass = 1.0 + 0.25 * rng.next_range(4) as f64;
    let contact_defect = (1 + rng.next_range(4)) as f64 / 512.0;
    let base_radius = 0.30 + 0.02 * rng.next_range(11) as f64;
    let eps = 0.8 + 0.05 * rng.next_range(9) as f64;
    let sigma = 0.02 + 0.01 * rng.next_range(12) as f64;
    let decay = 0.04 + 0.01 * rng.next_range(13) as f64;
    let n_elements = 60 + rng.next_range(61) as usize; // 60..=120
    let n_bands = 3 + rng.next_range(4) as u8;

    let mut naming_rng = Stream::new(seed, Domain::Naming, 0);
    let mut taken = Vec::new();
    let mut out: Vec<Element> = Vec::with_capacity(n_elements);

    for units in 1..=n_elements {
        // Which shell is filling, and how far into it.
        let (mut shell, mut filled) = (0_usize, 1_usize);
        while filled + packing::shell_size(k, shell + 1) <= units {
            shell += 1;
            filled += packing::shell_size(k, shell);
        }
        let outer = units - filled;
        let cap = packing::shell_size(k, shell + 1);
        let contacts = packing::contacts_upto(pack, units);

        let (symbol, name, _) = naming::mint(&mut naming_rng, &mut taken);

        // Mass, in sub-units of 1/1024, so the grid is a property of the
        // construction rather than a claim about it. `base_mass` is drawn on a
        // 0.25 step so `base_mass * 1024.0` is an exact integer; the defect is
        // quantised with `round_ties_even` to match `Mass`'s own convention —
        // ties are reachable (13 in this series, at N = 2, 3, 4, 7 among
        // others), and two conventions for one grid means a later "harmonise
        // the rounding" cleanup silently moves a golden.
        let base_sub = (base_mass * 1024.0).round_ties_even() as i64;
        let defect_sub = (contacts * contact_defect * 1024.0).round_ties_even() as i64;
        let mass = Mass::from_raw(units as i64 * base_sub - defect_sub);

        // Radius: the cube root of the unit count, times a bulge for the
        // partly-filled outer shell sitting proud of the packed core.
        //
        // G3: real atomic radius *decreases* across a period and jumps at a
        // new one. This does the opposite — grows through a shell, drops at
        // closure. Structurally anti-isomorphic, not a renamed trend.
        // **Radius is set by which shell is occupied, and it cannot shrink.** The
        // enclosing radius of a superset of units is never smaller, and an
        // earlier `cbrt(units) * (1 + 0.55*fill)` collapsed it 35% at every
        // closure (N=74 -> 75: 2.587 -> 1.687 on one unit added). Since G1
        // measured binding at ~93% size and ~7% shape, that cliff would have
        // read as closed-shell elements binding a different partner set — an
        // arithmetic accident wearing the look of interesting periodicity.
        //
        // For a shelled cluster the radius *is* the shell index: each shell adds
        // one lattice spacing. So `shell + fill` is both monotone and the better
        // model, and it recovers `N^(1/3)` asymptotically since N ~ k*shell^3/3.
        // Over the range a table actually spans the fitted exponent is
        // 0.29..0.32 against a true cube root's 0.333 — the `1.0 +` offset drags
        // it down. Not a defect; an unqualified range claim would be.
        //
        // G3 still holds, by a cleaner route: real atomic radius falls across a
        // period and jumps at a new one. This rises monotonically and never
        // jumps — structurally unlike, without being geometrically wrong.
        let fill = outer as f64 / cap as f64;
        let radius = base_radius * (1.0 + shell as f64 + fill);

        // Valence: docking notches on the frontier. Zero at a closure because
        // there is no frontier there.
        // `round_ties_even`, for the same reason as the mass defect: `notches`
        // lands on an exact 2.5 at k=8/outer=2 and k=8/outer=8, where ties-away
        // and ties-even differ by a whole bonding slot.
        let notches = packing::unmade_lateral(cap, outer) / packing::lateral_coordination(cap);
        let valence = notches.round_ties_even() as u8;

        // Affinity: exposed fraction of the cluster. Continuous across every
        // closure by construction — at `outer == 0` it reduces to the
        // outermost complete shell, and at `outer == cap` to the same
        // expression for the next one.
        let shell_units = if shell == 0 { 1 } else { packing::shell_size(k, shell) };
        let inner = filled - shell_units;
        let covered = shell_units as f64 * outer as f64 / cap as f64;
        let surface = units as f64 - (inner as f64 + covered);
        let affinity = 2.0 * (surface / units as f64) - 1.0;

        // Per-unit binding energy: made contacts, less radial strain.
        //
        // **The strain term is the invented destabiliser, and the obvious one
        // is a G3 breach.** The familiar way to bend a binding curve back down
        // is the semi-empirical mass formula's Coulomb term, and reaching for
        // it imports real nuclear physics with no data file in sight. This is a
        // different mechanism: a lattice cannot tile a sphere, so each shell is
        // stretched over a larger radius than the one below and carries a
        // strain growing as `n^2`, which competes with the contact term's
        // saturation and produces a maximum.
        //
        // (The defence "no charge, no isospin, no pairing and no asymmetry
        // term" stood here and is deleted: it enumerates the terms that are
        // *absent* and says nothing about the one that is present, which is the
        // shape of the `libm` doctrine error. §7.2's prose forbids it.)
        //
        // **The exponent 2/3 is CHOSEN, not derived, and it is load-bearing.**
        // Per-*shell* strain proportional to `n^2`, summed and divided by `N`,
        // gives `1/k` — a constant, not `N^(2/3)`. Reaching 2/3 needs
        // per-*site* strain proportional to `n^2`, which is a different claim.
        // Accumulating shell-by-shell instead — as `contacts_upto` does, for
        // the identical reason — moves the peak in **48.1% of drawn
        // universes**, so the `19 of 24` closure census is a property of this
        // choice and not of the packing. Deferred to Task 20 deliberately:
        // precedence 2, not a G3 breach, remedy known and it requotes every
        // number. Do not describe the exponent as derived.
        let energy_per_unit =
            eps * contacts / units as f64 - sigma * det_math::cbrt((units * units) as f64);

        // Abundance: fusion builds heavy clusters from light ones, so each
        // extra unit costs a step and abundance falls geometrically. Sequential
        // addition with a constant per-step survival gives `r^N = exp(N ln r)`.
        //
        // **There is deliberately no `exp(beta * energy_per_unit)` tilt.** A
        // draft had one at beta = 0.6. It pushed the *wrong way* — deleting it
        // moves nominal p_c from 0.358 to 0.381, better than the best the whole
        // `decay` sweep reaches. Its stated mechanism compounds to `E_total`,
        // not `E_per_unit`; implementing the stated story makes the heaviest
        // element the most abundant and collapses p_c to 0.112. And 0.6 was
        // typed in where every neighbouring constant is drawn, which is the
        // same shape of defect as `peak`-as-an-argument.
        //
        // **This is the gel lever (§7.2) and Task 4 gates on nothing.** Measured
        // nominal p_c is 0.47..0.91 across 24 universes (median 0.74) — the
        // figure `gel_band_holds` asserts, which is where to re-read it rather
        // than trusting this comment. Do NOT justify that with
        // "burial makes realised functionality lower, so realised p_c is higher"
        // — §7.2 carried that argument and it is wrong: under degree-independent
        // thinning the beaker gels at the same bonds per atom regardless, and
        // only the coordinate was rescaled. Report nominal f_w and
        // bonds-per-node at gel as diagnostics; the gate is Task 20's
        // battery, on `p_ss / p_c`. (A draft said Task 5, which creates
        // `bonds.rs` and has no battery and cannot have one.)
        let abundance = det_math::exp(-(units as f64) * decay);

        // Catalytic class: which band of outer-shell occupancy this element
        // exposes.
        //
        // **This is a rebinning of `(period, group)`, and the claim is narrowed
        // to say so.** A draft's comment here said Euler's twelve five-coordinate sites
        // mean a sparsely-filled shell exposes a different coordination mix from
        // a nearly-full one, "and that mix is what a mineral surface presents".
        // The code computed no such mix. Enumerated across all k, n_bands
        // and N, this takes exactly one value per `(outer, cap)` pair — zero
        // information beyond `group`. Two reviewers found it independently.
        //
        // So Task 4's headline is five §7.1 properties derived, plus
        // `abundance` — and this one honest rebinning, named as such.
        // Deriving it properly is possible — the five- versus six-coordinate
        // site mix on the frontier *is* computable, and depends on the filling
        // order — and is left to whoever needs it to mean something. Until then
        // nothing downstream may branch on it as though it carried chemistry.
        //
        // `outer < cap` by loop construction and `cap >= 8`, so this lands in
        // `0..n_bands` with neither a `max(1)` nor a trailing modulo.
        let outer_fill_band = ((outer * usize::from(n_bands)) / cap) as u8;

        out.push(Element {
            id: ElementId(out.len() as u8),
            symbol,
            name,
            units,
            period: shell as u8,
            group: outer as u8, // `outer < cap` and the table caps at 120
            mass,
            valence,
            affinity,
            radius: Span(radius),
            energy_per_unit,
            instability: 0.0, // filled below, once the peak is known
            abundance,
            outer_fill_band,
        });
    }

    // **The peak is read off here, after the series exists.** This ordering is
    // the mechanism: nothing above could have consulted it.
    //
    // Every closure is a *local* maximum by mechanism — a closed shell has no
    // frontier, so it makes every lateral contact available to it and maximises
    // contacts per unit, giving a sawtooth. That is structural: zero failures
    // across the full (k, eps, sigma) product.
    //
    // The **global** max is not. A series truncated mid-shell can peak at its
    // edge, and over the drawn table size it does in 4 of 24, with one further
    // universe peaking mid-shell. Measured: on a closure in 19 of 24, 13
    // distinct peaks. Both errors have been made here — an earlier version
    // called it a coincidence rate, and its correction called it structural
    // without qualification. Neither is right.
    let peak = out
        .iter()
        .fold((1_usize, f64::MIN), |(bn, be), e| {
            if e.energy_per_unit > be { (e.units, e.energy_per_unit) } else { (bn, be) }
        })
        .0;

    // Stability: distance below the peak, so the most tightly bound elements
    // persist and the extremes decay. One quantity, two consequences — which
    // is what the predecessor's two unlinked encodings could not give.
    let peak_energy = out.get(peak - 1).map_or(0.0, |e| e.energy_per_unit);
    for e in &mut out {
        // `f64::max` is disallowed — it returns either input on a tie and
        // measured `(+0.0).max(-0.0)` differs between aarch64 and x86-64.
        let scale = peak_energy.abs();
        let scale = if scale > f64::EPSILON { scale } else { f64::EPSILON };
        let deficit = (peak_energy - e.energy_per_unit) / scale;
        // **Cap at 1.0, and the 0.9 it replaces was never argued.**
        //
        // **The justification below is RETRACTED — see `element.rs` for the
        // current text.** It argued that `instability` "is a probability per
        // world-year (§7.1), so 1.0 is the value the type implies and needs no
        // defence". §7.1 did say that, and Task 15 hands the quantity to the
        // scheduler as a Gillespie propensity, which is unbounded above — so the
        // cap is a category error rather than a saturation, and Task 15 owns the
        // fix. A rename swept the new identifier into this paragraph, which made
        // a superseded argument read as a live one about the new name.
        //
        // The measurement below stands and is why 0.9 was wrong; only the
        // "needs no defence" half is retracted. 0.9 needed one and had none —
        // measured over the 2916-universe grid it binds on 1.19% of elements
        // but on **at least one element in every universe**, and those elements
        // sit at median position N/n_elements = 0.011: it is the monomer, the
        // feedstock, the most abundant species. So a bare 0.9 systematically
        // slowed the decay of the one species whose persistence matters most,
        // by up to 26%, as a side effect of a guard nobody described. The raw
        // deficit does exceed 1 (max measured 1.218), so a cap is genuinely
        // needed — it is the value that was wrong, not the clamp.
        //
        // If a future measurement wants the monomer to persist longer, that is
        // a physics claim about feedstock and belongs in §9.4 with the
        // measurement attached, not in a magic number here.
        e.instability = if deficit > 1.0 { 1.0 } else { deficit };
    }

    let shell = ShellPattern { k, closures: packing::closures(k, n_elements), peak };
    (shell, out)
}
```

Run: `cargo test --locked -p borbax-universe`
Expected: 24 passed, 1 panicking on the deliberate `todo!()` in
`frontier_matches_exact_counts_on_a_real_sphere` until `mod exact` is ported
from `fusion.rs`. Port it in this step — a green run with that test absent is
the state in which a 1.46x coefficient error stood undetected.

If `table_size_is_workable` fails, adjust the `n_elements` draw — not the

chemistry is too thin to be interesting, above ~120 the species space explodes
without adding anything.

If `valence_ceiling_is_four_to_six_over_the_drawn_range` fails, **do not widen

git commit -m "$(cat <<'EOF'

Element N is N copies of one base unit, packed into shells of k*n^2 + 2. Mass,
radius, valence, affinity, instability and abundance are all consequences of that
packing rather than curves chosen because they looked plausible — principle 2,
applied to the file that defines what an atom is. `outer_fill_band` is the one
property that is not, and it says so.

Only k is drawn. The n^2 is geometry and the +2 is the Euler characteristic,
so two universes differ in table *shape*, not just in the numbers filling one
shape.

Three properties the predecessor got wrong, each with the measurement:

- Valence was a fitted curve clamped at 6, and a per-site count cannot exceed

  with the coefficient fitted to exact counts on real triangulated spheres at

  Over the drawn table size the ceiling is 4..6. Valence 8 is not reachable,
  and that is not a shortfall: §7.2 asked for a thin high-valence tail, which
  is a shape claim, and the shape is present in every universe. Zero at a

  now read off the finished series and takes 13 distinct values across 24

  sits at a closure in 19 of 24, the misses being tables truncated mid-shell.
- Mass claimed to be exact on Mass's 1/1024 grid while being false for all 120

comment that used to claim a coordination mix the code never computed.

MrReasonable <4990954+MrReasonable@users.noreply.github.com>
EOF

### Task 5: Bond energies and the assembled Universe

**Files:**
- Create: `crates/borbax-universe/src/bonds.rs`
- Modify: `crates/borbax-universe/src/lib.rs`
- Test: both

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: `Element`, `ElementId`, `borbax_units::Quanta`
- Produces: `BondEnergyMatrix::energy(a, b, order) -> Quanta`, `UniverseConsts`, `PhysicsVersion`, `Universe` (carrying `physics: PhysicsVersion`), `Universe::generate(seed) -> Universe`, `Universe::generate_under(seed, PhysicsVersion) -> Universe`, `Universe::element(ElementId) -> &Element`

**`generate_under` is why the seam works, and it is easy to leave out.** A
universe is *derived* from its seed rather than serialised, so "reloading" one
means re-running generation. With only `generate(seed)`, once `V2` exists there
is no argument that could say which laws to apply and it will stamp whatever
the binary implements — the exact failure `physics` was added to prevent,
stated in that field's own test comment and unaddressed by its own interface
until this line. `generate(seed)` stays as `generate_under(seed, CURRENT)`, and
the address parser feeds `@1` into the other.

**Three decisions Task 4's review routed here, each with a discriminator.**

1. **`energy_per_unit` is a bare `f64` crossing a crate boundary, and Task 5 must
   decide that explicitly rather than inherit it.** §5's G4 is precedence 1 —
   absolute, nothing to trade against — and this is exactly the "bare `f64`
   carrying a quantity through three functions defeats this quietly" shape it
   exists to stop. `borbax-units` already provides `Div<f64> for Quanta`, so the
   type exists. Task 4 left it bare because the value is compared, summed and
   scaled inside `element.rs` where the newtype buys nothing; that argument does
   not extend past the boundary, and this task *is* the boundary.

   *Discriminator:* no energy-dimensioned quantity may cross `borbax-universe`'s
   public API as a bare `f64` — adding it to a `Span` or a `Thermal` must fail to
   compile. Deciding to keep the `f64` is a legitimate outcome; **inheriting it
   without deciding is not**, and the way to tell the difference is whether this
   step's commit message says which was chosen and why.

2. **`energy(a: &Element, b: &Element, order)` discards what `ElementId` is
   for.** A caller in a reaction-rate path holds `ElementId`s and would have to
   index the table to produce `&Element` before every lookup — coupling the rate
   loop to the element table's lifetime and to a struct 48 bytes of which are
   `String`. `energy(ElementId, ElementId, u8)` needs neither, and `ElementId` is
   documented in Task 4 as the newtype index that keeps `Mol12`'s atom array one
   byte per slot.

   *Discriminator:* the bond-energy lookup must be expressible without the
   element table in scope. Adding an `ElementId` overload *alongside* the
   `&Element` one does not satisfy it — the `&Element` form remaining is what
   lets a hot path pick it up.

3. **`ElementId` is write-only until this task, and the shape it lands in is
   `elements[id.0 as usize]`** — a panic site at every call site, in a workspace
   where `indexing_slicing` is `warn` under `-D warnings`. Task 5's first act
   would otherwise be an `#[expect]` per call site, or a `get()` with nothing to
   return. One struct closes it: `PeriodicTable { pattern, elements }` with
   `get(ElementId) -> Option<&Element>` and `iter() -> (ElementId, &Element)`,
   which also names the pair `generate_elements` currently returns as a bare
   tuple and gives `ElementId`'s field a reason to go private.

   *Discriminator:* a downstream crate must not be able to turn an `ElementId`
   into a `usize` index without a fallible lookup. "The tuple got a name" does
   not satisfy it.

**Task 4's rewrite changed this task's inputs, and the steps below have not been
updated for it.** `Element` gained `units`, `energy_per_unit` and `abundance`;
`period`/`group` are now the shell index and outer-shell occupancy rather than
drawn coordinates; `ShellPattern` carries `k`, `closures` and `peak` instead of
a `periods: Vec<u8>`. Reconcile the code below against `element.rs` before
executing, rather than assuming the field names still line up.

**One thing already resolved, recorded so it is not re-opened.** A draft of Task
4 made `generate_elements` fallible, because `Mass::from_f64_quantised` returns
a `Result`, and deferred a ~46-call-site signature change here as an open
choice. `Mass::from_raw(i64)` exists — public, `const`, infallible — and the
fusion mass is an exact integer count of 1/1024ths by construction, verified
bit-for-bit over 240 000 masses with 5 014× headroom. Task 4 builds the mass in
sub-units, so there is no error to propagate and nothing here changes.

**Carried from Task 4: bond energy must be a function of `Element::energy_per_unit`,
not an independently drawn matrix.** Task 4 derives one binding energy per unit
from the packing counts and makes both the mass defect and the stability
functions of it, precisely because its predecessor encoded "these bind well"
twice in incommensurable units — so neither encoding could check the other, and
the "emergent" stability minimum turned out to be the distance from a typed-in
constant. Drawing `bond_energies` independently reproduces that defect one level
up, and §7.2 puts the reactivity and decay bands here, which are the two most
likely to kill a universe.

Task 4's claim is deliberately scoped to the properties Task 4 computes.
**`bond_energies` is the one substantive §7.1 property still without a cause,
and this task is where it acquires one.** The distinguishing test is not that a
matrix exists but that it *moves*: perturbing `energy_per_unit` for one element
must change every bond energy involving it, and a test that passes with the
matrix drawn from its own stream is not testing this.

#[cfg(test)]
mod tests {
    use super::*;
    use crate::element::generate_elements;


    #[test]
    fn energies_are_symmetric() {
        let (_, els) = generate_elements(4);
        let m = BondEnergyMatrix::generate(&els, &mut crate::element::stream(4));
        for a in 0..els.len().min(20) {
            for b in 0..els.len().min(20) {
                assert_eq!(m.energy(&els[a], &els[b], 1), m.energy(&els[b], &els[a], 1));
            }
        }
    }

    #[test]
    fn higher_orders_are_stronger() {
        let (_, els) = generate_elements(4);
        let m = BondEnergyMatrix::generate(&els, &mut crate::element::stream(4));
        let (a, b) = (&els[3], &els[7]);
        assert!(m.energy(a, b, 2) > m.energy(a, b, 1));
        assert!(m.energy(a, b, 3) > m.energy(a, b, 2));
    }

    #[test]
    fn energies_are_positive_and_finite() {
        let (_, els) = generate_elements(9);
        let m = BondEnergyMatrix::generate(&els, &mut crate::element::stream(9));
        for a in &els {
            for b in &els {
                let e = m.energy(a, b, 1);
                assert!(e.get() > 0.0 && e.is_finite(), "{} - {} = {e:?}", a.symbol, b.symbol);
            }
        }
    }

    /// Spread matters: a matrix where every bond costs the same gives a
    /// chemistry with no structure to exploit. Some linkages must be durable
    /// and others fragile, or decay has nothing to select on (spec §22.6).
    #[test]
    fn energies_have_useful_spread() {
        let (_, els) = generate_elements(9);
        let m = BondEnergyMatrix::generate(&els, &mut crate::element::stream(9));
        let all: Vec<f64> =
            els.iter().flat_map(|a| els.iter().map(move |b| (a, b))).map(|(a, b)| m.energy(a, b, 1).get()).collect();
        let lo = all.iter().copied().fold(f64::MAX, f64::min);
        let hi = all.iter().copied().fold(f64::MIN, f64::max);
        assert!(hi / lo > 3.0, "bond energies too uniform: {lo}..{hi}");
    }
}

```rust
//! Generated bond energies (spec §7.1).
//!
//! Energy is a function of the two elements' *groups*, not their identities.
//! That is deliberate: it means bond strength is another property families
//! share, so "elements in this column form fragile bonds" is a true and
//! discoverable statement rather than noise.

use crate::element::Element;
use borbax_rng::Stream;
use borbax_units::Quanta;

#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
pub struct BondEnergyMatrix {
    n_groups: usize,
    /// Row-major, `n_groups * n_groups`, symmetric by construction.
    e: Vec<f64>,
    /// Multipliers for bond order 1, 2, 3.
    order_scale: [f64; 3],
}

impl BondEnergyMatrix {
    #[must_use]
    pub fn generate(elements: &[Element], rng: &mut Stream) -> Self {
        let n_groups = elements.iter().map(|e| e.group as usize).max().unwrap_or(0) + 1;
        let mut e = vec![0.0; n_groups * n_groups];

        let base = rng.next_f64_range(30.0, 90.0);
        // Complementary affinities bond strongly; like affinities bond weakly.
        // This is what ties bonding to the same affinity scalar that drives
        // surface complementarity in §8.3, rather than inventing a second axis.
        let polarity_weight = rng.next_f64_range(0.4, 1.2);

        // Mean affinity per group, so energy depends on the group's character.
        let mut group_affinity = vec![0.0; n_groups];
        let mut group_count = vec![0u32; n_groups];
        for el in elements {
            group_affinity[el.group as usize] += el.affinity;
            group_count[el.group as usize] += 1;
        }
        for g in 0..n_groups {
            if group_count[g] > 0 {
                group_affinity[g] /= f64::from(group_count[g]);
            }
        }

        for a in 0..n_groups {
            for b in a..n_groups {
                let opposition = -(group_affinity[a] * group_affinity[b]); // in [-1, 1]
                let noise = rng.next_normal() * 0.08;
                let v = base * (1.0 + polarity_weight * opposition + noise);
                let v = v.max(base * 0.15); // never zero, never negative
                e[a * n_groups + b] = v;
                e[b * n_groups + a] = v;
            }
        }

        let order_scale = [1.0, rng.next_f64_range(1.6, 2.1), rng.next_f64_range(2.2, 3.0)];
        Self { n_groups, e, order_scale }
    }

    /// Bond dissociation energy for a bond of the given order.
    /// Orders outside 1..=3 clamp rather than panic — a malformed molecule
    /// should be inert, not a crash.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn energy(&self, a: &Element, b: &Element, order: u8) -> Quanta {
        let (ga, gb) = (a.group as usize, b.group as usize);
        if ga >= self.n_groups || gb >= self.n_groups {
            return Quanta::ZERO;
        }
        let scale = self.order_scale[(order.clamp(1, 3) - 1) as usize];
        Quanta(self.e[ga * self.n_groups + gb] * scale)
    }
}
```

- [x] **Step 4: Implement the Universe**

**The firewall this establishes, which is worth stating once and obeying
forever.** Two kinds of change look similar in a diff and are completely
different in consequence:

| Change | Touches | Effect on existing worlds |
|---|---|---|
| `affinity()`, folding, decay, generation | **physics** | every species in every world changes — needs a `PhysicsVersion` bump |
| world generator, environment, niche machinery, scaffolding | scaffolding — compatible, still subject to §3.6 | no effect on existing **chemistry**; world generation is a separate, currently unversioned breakage |

**§3.6 applies identically on both sides of this line. Being above the
chemistry buys compatibility, not permission.** The table's third column says
what it measures — effect on existing worlds — and nothing more. A
world-generator change that made condensation favourable wherever a template is
present sits in row two, needs no version bump, breaks no world, and is exactly
what §3.6 forbids.

That distinction was got wrong here on the first attempt, in a way worth
recording. §11 names POET and Minimal Criterion Coevolution as the candidates
for V3's niche machinery, and an earlier version of this section pre-cleared
them as "environmental: they generate challenges, they do not alter what a
molecule is". True, and not the test. The test is whether the machinery is
*conditioned on the outcome it exists to make probable* — and for both of these
it is, definitionally:

> POET: *"filters out child environments that appear too challenging or too
> trivial **for the current level of capability of agents**."*
>
> MCC: any maze that is **solved at least once** may reproduce.

The environment population's admission rule reads agent performance. Strip that
minimal criterion and what remains is random environment mutation, which is
what §11 already assumes will not work — so the machinery cannot be adopted
without adopting the conditioning. Transposed to Borbax: generate a tidal-flat
variant, keep it if the current chemistry *almost* produces a replicator. That
is "condensation is favoured when a template is present" implemented one level
up, where it is harder to see. The dice are not loaded; they are re-rolled
until they read the number we wanted.

§11's own wording is *"should be evaluated then, not assumed away now"*, and
that stands. If POET is wanted later, its minimal criterion has to be replaced
by something outcome-blind — environments admitted on an intrinsic diversity or
novelty-of-*conditions* measure, never on what the chemistry achieved in them.
The §15.3 neutral shadow does not rescue it either: a shadow run under the same
filtered environment stream inherits the same conditioning.

Voltage-gated transport is the awkward counter-example, and it is worse than a
version bump. If folding depends on a bulk field then **fold stops being a pure
function of the species** — it becomes a function of (species, local field),
which in the molecular tier is per-location and effectively per-molecule. That
is the §8.6 regression CLAUDE.md warns "arrives disguised as a small feature",
here disguised as a physics extension and nearly waved through as "fine".

There is a clean mitigation and it must be a stated precondition of that
version rather than a discovery made when the fold cache starts missing:
quantise the field into a small fixed number of bands and key the species cache
on `(species, band)`. Still per-species × a constant, and the intern-time work
stays intern-time.

Record the physics-level statement, too, not the phenomenon name. "Voltage-gated
transport" names a real biological mechanism, and the name is what gets
implemented in two years — it invites `if species.is_pore { permeability *= f(v) }`,
a species-classification branch. *"An environmental scalar enters the fold
energy for every species"* cannot be written that way.

`crates/borbax-universe/src/lib.rs`:

```rust
//! A generated universe: the laws of physics for one `universe_seed`.
//!
//! Generated once, then immutable. Everything downstream — worlds, molecules,
//! reactions — reads these constants and never changes them.

pub mod bonds;
pub mod element;
pub mod naming;

pub use bonds::BondEnergyMatrix;
pub use element::{Element, ElementId, ShellPattern};

use borbax_rng::{Domain, Stream};
use borbax_units::Thermal;

/// Universe-wide constants. Every one is generated, so two universes differ
/// in character rather than merely in their random draws.
#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
pub struct UniverseConsts {
    /// Target separation for a complementary fit in §8.3. Two surfaces
    /// interlock when their extents sum to about this.
    pub ideal_gap: f64,
    /// Relative weight of geometric fit vs surface-character opposition.
    pub w_shape: f64,
    pub w_charge: f64,
    /// Pre-exponential factor in the Arrhenius rate law (§9.1).
    pub rate_prefactor: f64,
    /// Global spontaneous-cleavage scale. V0 locates the decay band by
    /// moving this single number (spec §22.6); per-bond rates come later.
    pub decay_scale: f64,
    /// Which element acts as solvent. Its complementarity against a bond
    /// determines solvent attack (§9.5).
    pub solvent: ElementId,
    pub temp_min: Thermal,
    pub temp_max: Thermal,
}

/// The laws a universe was generated under.
///
/// **A universe is `(seed, physics)`, not `seed`.** §6 promises that
/// sharing `U-7F3A21C9` means the recipient gets the same planet; today the
/// real identity is `(seed, git SHA, rustc version)` and only the first third
/// is written down. This field writes down the rest of what matters.
///
/// The cost is stated honestly: every version here is a code path kept alive
/// forever, with its own goldens. That is the price of never breaking a world
/// somebody cared about, and it is bounded — versions are added when physics
/// changes, which should be roughly never.
///
/// **Gate on this, never on a zero weight.** The tempting shortcut for an
/// added term is to give it weight `0.0` for old universes and claim they are
/// unchanged. They are not: `0.0 * x` is `NaN` when `x` is infinite or NaN,
/// and a stray NaN's sign bit differs between the CI architectures (see
/// `det_math`). An inert term must be *skipped*:
///
/// ```text
/// if u.physics >= PhysicsVersion::V2 { score += w_field * field_term; }   // yes
/// score += u.consts.w_field * field_term;                                 // no
/// ```
///
/// New constants for a new version come from a **new `Domain`** (Task 3), so
/// adding them leaves every draw an existing universe already made untouched.
/// Those two rules together are what make a later law additive rather than
/// destructive.
///
/// # The admission criterion, which is not the same as the cost
///
/// This documents what a new version *costs* and offers no rule for when one
/// is *allowed*, and that gap is load-bearing. Before the seam existed, adding
/// a law carried a deterrent that was policing §3.1 by accident: it invalidated
/// every world ever generated, which is a visible, expensive, argued-about
/// event. Now it is a checklist — new `Domain`, new variant, regenerate
/// goldens. The seam is right (a world silently changing meaning is worse), but
/// it removed friction that a different invariant was relying on.
///
/// The `if physics >= V2 { score += w * term }` idiom makes it sharper: that is
/// monotone accretion inside `affinity()`, and two versions in, the scoring
/// function is a list of version-gated additive terms that all look alike.
/// "V2 physics has a term that helps membranes form" would be one more line
/// among them.
///
/// **So: a new `PhysicsVersion` must state the physical process it adds in
/// terms that name no biological structure.** "An environmental scalar enters
/// the fold energy" passes. "A rule that helps membranes form" fails on its
/// face, because it names its own outcome. That is §3.1's deletion test applied
/// to the justification rather than the code, and it is the thing that makes
/// this seam safe to have.
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, PartialOrd, Ord, Hash)]
#[non_exhaustive]
pub enum PhysicsVersion {
    /// Shape and surface character, per spec §8.3. Everything in V0 and V1.
    V1 = 1,
}

#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
pub struct Universe {
    pub seed: u64,
    /// The laws this universe was generated under. Never inferred from the
    /// running binary — a universe loaded from disk keeps the physics it was
    /// born with, or it is a different universe wearing the same name.
    pub physics: PhysicsVersion,
    pub shell: ShellPattern,
    pub elements: Vec<Element>,
    pub bonds: BondEnergyMatrix,
    pub consts: UniverseConsts,
}

impl Universe {
    #[must_use]
    pub fn generate(seed: u64) -> Self {
        // `generate_elements` returns the shell it actually used. An earlier
        // draft re-derived it by rebuilding the same stream and replaying —
        // which agreed by luck, and would silently stop agreeing the moment
        // anyone added a draw before the shell call inside `generate_elements`.
        // `Universe.shell` would then describe a table that does not exist,
        // with nothing failing. This is the class of bug dropping `Copy` on
        // `Stream` (Task 3) exists to surface.
        let (shell, elements) = element::generate_elements(seed);

        // A separate stream index so that adding a draw in element generation
        // cannot shift the constants, and vice versa.
        let mut rng = Stream::new(seed, Domain::Universe, 1);
        let bonds = BondEnergyMatrix::generate(&elements, &mut rng);

        // Solvent: a small, abundant, strongly-polar element. Picking from the
        // first third biases toward light elements without hardcoding one.
        let solvent_pool = (elements.len() / 3).max(1);
        let solvent = ElementId(rng.next_range(solvent_pool as u64) as u8);

        let temp_min = Thermal(rng.next_f64_range(180.0, 260.0));
        let temp_max = temp_min + Thermal(rng.next_f64_range(200.0, 500.0));

        let consts = UniverseConsts {
            ideal_gap: rng.next_f64_range(1.6, 2.8),
            w_shape: rng.next_f64_range(0.6, 1.4),
            w_charge: rng.next_f64_range(0.6, 1.4),
            rate_prefactor: rng.next_f64_range(1e3, 1e5),
            decay_scale: rng.next_f64_range(1e-6, 1e-4),
            solvent,
            temp_min,
            temp_max,
        };

        Self {
            seed,
            // Set at construction, never inferred from the running binary.
            // A universe reloaded under newer laws must keep the ones it was
            // born with, or `U-7F3A21C9` stops meaning what §6 promises.
            physics: PhysicsVersion::V1,
            shell,
            elements,
            bonds,
            consts,
        }
    }

    #[must_use]
    pub fn element(&self, id: ElementId) -> &Element {
        // Callers hold ids minted by this universe; an out-of-range id is a
        // programming error, so clamping keeps the type total without hiding
        // it behind an Option that every call site would have to unwrap.
        &self.elements[(id.0 as usize).min(self.elements.len() - 1)]
    }

    #[must_use]
    pub fn solvent(&self) -> &Element {
        self.element(self.consts.solvent)
    }
}
```

Run: `cargo test -p borbax-universe`
Expected: 50 passed (the whole crate — Task 4 shipped 48 of them, and the
number here said 19 because it predates Task 4's rewrite).

**What actually shipped differs from Steps 1, 3 and 4 above, which were never
updated for Task 4's rewrite — as this task's own preamble warned.** Recorded
here rather than by editing the blocks, so the reasoning stays legible:

- **The matrix is keyed by `ElementId`, not `group`, and is derived from
  `energy_per_unit`, not affinity opposition.** Step 3's code satisfies neither
  the preamble's mandate nor its discriminator: it never reads
  `energy_per_unit`, so perturbing one element's binding energy changes no bond
  at all. Its two stated mechanisms are also false against the table Task 4
  generates — `affinity` has **no negative half** (measured: `[0.140476, 1.0]`,
  0 negatives over 36 013 elements), so `-(a·b)` is negative for every pair and
  says "high affinity bonds weakly", not "complementary affinities bond
  strongly"; and at most **4 elements share a `group`**, so keying on it aliases
  unrelated elements rather than naming a family. `bonds.rs` documents both with
  the measurement, and `the_table_still_justifies_ignoring_affinity_and_group`
  fails if either stops being true.
- **The per-pair `next_normal() * 0.08` noise is gone.** It is an independently
  drawn matrix in the exact proportion of the noise, which is what the preamble
  forbids.
- **`order: u8` became `BondOrder`.** Clamping `0` up to `1` is not "inert" —
  it hands a non-bond the energy of a single bond.
- **The first derivation had a real defect, caught by the discriminator test
  and worth keeping in the record.** Normalising binding energy onto `[0, 1]`
  gives the weakest element capacity exactly zero, and zero is absorbing under a
  geometric mean — so every bond to it scored identically regardless of partner.
  That element is reliably the monomer (`contacts_upto(1) = 0`), the feedstock
  and most abundant species, so the commonest bond in the chemistry had no
  structure at all. The whole-matrix spread test could not see it. Fixed by
  putting the floor on the *capacity* scale instead of on the finished energy.

Measured over seeds 0..64: single-bond energies span `[4.911, 89.935]` Quanta,
and the within-universe max/min ratio is `[3.578, 8.263]` — which is exactly
`1 / weakest_share`, asserted by
`the_spread_is_exactly_the_reciprocal_of_the_weakest_share`.

### Task 5b: Abundance as an epoch fast-forward of Borbax's own assembly

> ## SCAFFOLDING — specified, not scheduled. Do NOT execute this as part of V0.
>
> **V0 keeps `exp(-units * decay)`.** This task is a real improvement and it is
> fully specified below, but it is not on V0's critical path and executing it now
> would be scope creep against the MVP.
>
> **Three facts, and they agree:**
>
> 1. **It gates nothing.** No other task references Task 5b. Grep confirms it.
> 2. **The abundance V0 already ships is healthy.** Measured over 2000 seeds,
>    abundance-weighted `f_w > 2` — Molloy-Reed's existence criterion, so polymers
>    are possible — in **2000 of 2000** universes, median 2.4428, **p5 2.0993** so
>    even the bottom of the distribution clears it, median `p_c` 0.6931. §7.2 is
>    satisfied by the one-line profile. This task is an enhancement, not a fix.
> 3. **Its own Step 5 cannot run yet.** It re-measures the operating point, and
>    §7.2 requires `p_ss` be *counted* in a beaker rather than evaluated from the
>    closed form. There is no beaker until Task 19.
>
> And a fourth, which is the practical one: this task's three free parameters
> (`k0`, `d0`, `epochs`) can only be **located** by §7.2's battery, which is Task
> 20. Choosing them before the battery exists means choosing them by eye — the
> exact failure the battery was built to prevent, and the reason CLAUDE.md calls
> the decay band the first thing to suspect when a run produces nothing.
>
> **Sequence it after the beaker runs**, when its discriminators can be measured
> instead of asserted. At that point the parameters are a sweep, not a guess.
>
> Everything below stays as written — that is the point of scaffolding. The
> measurements are real, the blockers are real, and re-deriving them later would
> cost more than reading them.


**Files:** modify `crates/borbax-universe/src/element.rs`; test same file.

**Interfaces:** `Element::abundance` **moves off `Element`.** It becomes
`abundance(universe, epochs) -> Vec<f64>` — see the mechanism below. That is the
signature change §7.1 has been asking for: `abundance` is not in §7.1's element
property table at all, and §11 lists "element abundances" as V1 *world*-gen
("which of the universe's elements are common **here**"). It was on the wrong
type.

**This is a deliberate physics change and a golden regeneration.** Both digests
move. It is its own task rather than part of Task 5 because Task 5 took six
review rounds against the current abundance, and a physics change landing
mid-review of a different one is how findings get measured against stale code.

## The mechanism

`abundance` is currently `exp(-units * decay)` — one drawn constant, strictly
monotone decreasing, no structure.

**It becomes the state of a fusion process run forward for a specified number of
epochs.** Every unit of matter starts as a monomer. Each epoch, clusters fuse
**pairwise** — `A_M + A_N -> A_{M+N}`, both originals consumed, one product — at
a rate gated on whether the fusion is **exothermic**: the product must be more
tightly bound in total than the two parts,

```
Q(M, N) = (M+N)*E(M+N) - M*E(M) - N*E(N) > 0
```

where `E` is `energy_per_unit`, which the table already computes. Run for
`epochs`. The abundance vector is where the mass ended up.

**Mass is conserved by construction in the reals, and to a few ulps in `f64`** —
`M + N = M+N`, with nothing created and nothing left over. But
`M*flux + N*flux != (M+N)*flux` in binary floating point: measured over 60 seeds
at 400 epochs with a pinned ascending accumulation, `sum(N*a(N))` is
**bit-identical to 1.0 in only 18 of 60**, worst deviation `6.66e-16` — **3
ulps**. A ten-decimal readout cannot see that, and an earlier version of this
sentence said "exactly and by construction" on the strength of one.

**Do not write `assert_eq!(mass, 1.0)`.** CLAUDE.md reserves "exact by
construction" for `Mass`, which is fixed-point `i64`. Measured over 200 seeds x
2000 epochs, sampled every 25: worst **25 ulps** at `k0 = 1.0`, 13 at 0.5, and
bit-exactly 1.0 in only **3404 of 16 000 samples** — so `assert_eq!` fails four
times in five. The error is *bounded, not drifting*: it bounces within +/-25 ulps
out to 2000 epochs. Assert `|mass - 1.0| <= 32.0 * f64::EPSILON` — headroom over a
measurement, and still tight enough to catch a stoichiometry error, which is
`O(1)` rather than `O(eps)`.

Pin the direction of the checking sum too: ascending and descending differ in
**11 140 of 16 000** samples (§13.1).

*What that assertion also decides, for free:* the plan does not say what happens
to a pair whose product exceeds the table. Dropping the pair (a reflecting edge)
conserves mass; consuming the reactants and dropping the product leaks it;
clamping the product to `n_elements` creates it. The mass assertion distinguishes
all three, so state which is intended and let the test hold it. *Whether* a
reflecting edge is the right physics is an emergence question, not a numerics
one.

If exactness is actually wanted, do the flux bookkeeping in `Mass` — a real
option, and what the invariant deserves if it is load-bearing. This matters because two weaker
formulations were tried first and both failed:

| formulation | what went wrong |
|---|---|
| linear "shift mass up one bin", `M^epochs` by squaring | conserves *cluster count*, not mass — mass grew by a factor of `n_elements`, and count conservation holds for **every** column-stochastic matrix, so the assertion could not fail |
| sequential monomer addition `A_N + A_1 -> A_{N+1}` | self-limiting but **stalls**: the monomer reservoir exhausts, and measured over 1e6 epochs the mass-weighted mean cluster size reaches **2.95**, never leaving the light end |

Pairwise fusion has neither failure. It is also the only one of the three whose
rate vanishes as its reactants deplete, so no bin can go negative without a
clamp — the linear-with-debit variant reached **-0.590** at 5 epochs.

### The channel set is every pair, and that decides the criterion

**`M` and `N` range over all cluster sizes, including `M = 1`.** That is the
rule as stated — *every* pair fuses — and it has a consequence that must be
written down here rather than discovered in Step 3.

**An earlier version of this block reversed the "peak is NOT the cutoff"
decision, and that was wrong.** The reasoning was: `peak = argmax E(N)` is the
criterion for *symmetric* assembly, pairwise fusion is symmetric assembly,
therefore `peak` is the criterion. The middle step is false. A kernel over all
pairs **contains** `A_N + A_1 -> A_{N+1}` — which is exactly the sequential
monomer addition the original block analysed and the reversal claimed to retire.
So the sequential reading was never replaced, and the marginal `D(N)` analysis
above stands. **The "peak is NOT the cutoff" block is reinstated.**

This is not a quibble; the two channels behave completely differently, and a
single "is any pair exothermic" number reports only the weakest one. Measured
over 500 seeds — **with each definition stated, because two independent
measurements of "the symmetric channel" disagreed until they were spelled out:**

| frontier, defined as | short of the table edge |
|---|---|
| any pair, largest exothermic **step** product | **22 / 500 — 4.4%** |
| any pair, **reachability closure** from the monomer | 29 / 500 — 5.8% |
| symmetric only, largest exothermic **step** product | 399 / 500 — 79.8% |
| symmetric only, **reachability closure** from the monomer | 494 / 500 — 98.8% |

The histogram behind it: the smallest `M` for which the top element is
exothermically reachable is **`M = 1` in 475 of 500** universes. The monomer
channel is what carries mass to the top, and it outruns the symmetric channel at
every parameter setting — the symmetric frontier scales as `(eps/sigma)^(1/p)`
while the sequential one scales as `(eps/sigma)^(3/2)` at `p = 2/3`.

**Which means the model must be channel-aware or say which channel it means.**
Two coherent options, and Step 3 must pick one in a sentence:

1. *All pairs* (the rule as stated). Then the operative cutoff is the **marginal**
   one, `peak` is not it, and the "no derived cutoff in 95.6%" result stands as a
   fact about the marginal criterion.
2. *Symmetric assembly.* Then restrict the kernel — a size-ratio condition, or
   `min(M, N) >= N_min` — and the exponent is already doing its job, with an
   interior barrier in 79.8% of universes today.

**And the strain-exponent attribution was wrong, which matters because it routed
a modelling decision to Task 20 as a numerics item.** The exponent needed to make
the *any-pair* frontier interior is `p ~ 0.9-1.0` (measured: at `p = 0.8`,
244/500 are short of the edge; at `p = 1.0`, 452/500). That is **not** defensible
from packing geometry — per-*site* strain proportional to `n^2` integrates to
total `N^(5/3)`, i.e. per-unit `N^(2/3)`, which is what the code has; `p = 1`
needs per-site strain proportional to `n^3` with no story behind it. The strain
exponent remains an open item for its own reasons (it moves the peak in 48.1% of
universes); it is **not** the cause of the missing any-pair cutoff, and a
reviewer who "fixes" it will change nothing observable here.

### Fission is the reverse channel, and `instability` already drives it

**Fusion alone has no reverse, so it has no equilibrium.** Run it far enough and
every universe converges on whatever the irreversible process absorbs into —
measured, the table edge — which is why `epochs` was the only thing bounding the
climb, why the "successive differences shrink" assertion failed in the operating
regime, and why the heavy end had to be bounded by a stated ceiling. A review put
it exactly: with no fission channel, detailed balance on this kernel gives a
Boltzmann weight in total binding, **which is the distribution this design
already recorded as rejected**.

**The channel already exists and was not being used.** `Element::instability` is
derived from distance below the binding-energy peak — 0 at the peak, rising
toward both extremes. Adding
the reverse of fusion, `A_{M+N} -> A_M + A_N` at a rate driven by
`instability(M+N)`, is **one mechanism run both ways** (§8.3) rather than a
second one.

Measured, 40 seeds, `k0 = 0.5`, mass-weighted argmax against the binding peak:

| fission strength `d0` | epochs | mass peaks at N | binding peak | mass error |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 (fusion only) | 400 | 64.5 | 47.5 | 6.7e-16 |
| 0 | 6400 | **66.2** | 47.5 | 2.2e-15 |
| 0.2 | 1600 | **50.5** | 47.5 | — |
| 0.5 | 1600 | 22.8 | 47.5 | 9.1e-15 |
| 0.5 | 6400 | 22.8 | 47.5 | 3.1e-14 |

**Mass stays conserved** through both channels (worst 3.1e-14 at 6400 epochs,
consistent with the ulp envelope above — fission is the exact reverse of fusion,
so the stoichiometry is symmetric by construction).

> ## RETRACTED — the headline claim did not survive a peak-blind control
>
> **"The peak of the mass distribution moves toward the *binding* peak" is not
> evidence that `instability` did it.** A fragmentation rate that cannot see the
> binding peak produces the same crossing under the same sweep. Measured on
> Borbax's own tables, 24 seeds, 400 epochs, mass-weighted argmax:
>
> | fragmentation rate | `d0`=0 | 0.05 | 0.1 | 0.2 | 0.5 |
> |---|---|---|---|---|---|
> | `instability` | 67.2 | 68.8 | 67.1 | **46.2** | 19.4 |
> | **constant** — peak-blind | 67.2 | **1.0** | 1.0 | 1.0 | 1.0 |
> | **linear in size** — peak-blind | 67.2 | **22.7** | 13.1 | 10.0 | 6.8 |
>
> Mean binding peak 47.4. All three cross it. **Any** monotone one-parameter
> family running from "no fission" (mass at the table edge) to "strong fission"
> (mass at the monomer) must pass through the binding peak on the way, because
> the peak is between those endpoints. Landing on it is a property of the sweep
> having a root, not of the rate function knowing where the peak is.
>
> So `d0 = 0.2` giving 46.2 against 47.4 is a **root-find reported as a
> consequence** — the same defect `generate_elements` already records for `peak`
> as an argument, one level up. Retracted.
>
> **And a second, harder control found the real mechanism — it is the fusion
> gate, and it pre-dates this commit.** Delete fission entirely and the
> peak-tracking gets *stronger*. Measured over 200 seeds, correlation between
> the mass argmax and the binding peak, against a peak standard deviation of
> 24.5:
>
> | model | argmax mean | r(argmax, peak) | mean abs error to peak |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | **fusion only, gate ON** | 63.51 | **0.786** | **14.03** |
> | + fission `d0 = 0.2` (the headline) | 47.03 | 0.497 | 21.95 |
> | + fission `d0 = 0.5` | 19.27 | 0.233 | 34.97 |
> | fusion only, **gate OFF** | 88.67 | **0.037** | 39.20 |
>
> With the gate off the argmax pins at the table edge in every seed
> (`r` with `n_elements` = 1.000). **So `Q(M,N) > 0` is the entire peak-tracking
> mechanism; fission degrades it, monotonically in `d0`.** And rate-matched
> against a constant `instability`, the constant *wins*: `r = 0.811`, error
> 13.47, against the real field's 0.499 and 22.15.
>
> **Say the true thing instead, because it is stronger than the claim it
> replaces:** *the fusion exothermicity gate alone puts the mass argmax near the
> binding peak — r = 0.786, mean error 14.0 against a peak spread of 24.5.* That
> is a genuine emergent result, it needs no fission, and it was already true
> before this channel existed.
>
> What fission actually buys is arresting the front (see the steady-state
> finding below). Justify it on that.
>
> *Discriminator, and it must be run before this block is believed again:* sweep
> `d0` with `frag(s) = 1.0` and with `frag(s) = s/n`. If either lands the argmax
> near the peak, `instability` is not what put it there.

> ## The stated mechanism is inverted
>
> This block said "heavy clusters are the *least* stable, so fission removes them
> fastest". **Backwards.** `instability` is 0 at the peak and rises toward *both*
> extremes, and `energy_per_unit` is nearly flat past the peak (measured
> elsewhere in this task: 4.4404 at peak 60 to 4.2958 at N=95 on seed 0), so the
> heavy side barely rises while the light side rises steeply.
>
> Measured over 500 drawn universes: the light quarter's mean `instability`
> exceeds the above-peak mean in **444 of 444** universes with an interior peak —
> 100.0%, no exceptions.
>
> **And the flux measurement settles it.** Fission mass flux by size quintile at
> the state this block calls equilibrium (200 seeds, `d0 = 0.5`, 1600 epochs):
>
> | Q1 (lightest) | Q2 | Q3 | Q4 | Q5 (heaviest) | above the peak |
> |---|---|---|---|---|---|
> | **86.8%** | 6.6% | 2.9% | 1.9% | **1.9%** | **10.5%** |
>
> Meanwhile Q5 holds 14.2% of the mass. So the heavy end is bounded because the
> **light** end is being shredded back to monomer, starving the cascade — not
> because heavy clusters are removed. Three of four split rules collapse to the
> monomer for the same reason.
>
> That falsifies the bullet below claiming the heavy end is bounded without a
> ceiling: the heavy end is the part fission touches *least*, and the ceiling the
> old design carried was doing real work.

> ## Four more, from the coagulation–fragmentation literature
>
> - **Detailed balance is violated, and the recorded reason for the channel is
>   backwards.** This is the discrete Smoluchowski coagulation–fragmentation
>   system (Ball & Carr, *J. Stat. Phys.* 61 (1990) 203; convergence under
>   detailed balance, Cañizo, *J. Stat. Phys.* 129 (2007) 1,
>   doi:10.1007/s10955-007-9373-2). The class has a **closed-form** equilibrium
>   only under detailed balance, which requires `ln(a/b)/Q` to be one constant
>   across every channel. Measured on the planned kernel: that ratio spans
>   **0.324 to 3837.9**, and **26.8% of channel traffic is net circulating flux**
>   at the claimed steady state (it is 1.3e-15 for a detailed-balanced kernel).
>   So the measured "equilibrium" is a *non*-equilibrium steady state whose
>   location no theorem predicts.
>
>   And this block's stated reason — "without fission the limit is a Boltzmann
>   weight in total binding" — is inverted. With no fission there is **no**
>   positive detailed-balance equilibrium at all; pure coagulation is
>   irreversible and has no Gibbs measure. The Boltzmann weight is what a
>   *correctly reversed* channel produces. Which matters, because
>   `element.rs` records that shape as killed on measurement.
>
>   The construction that does satisfy it: `B(i,j) = A(i,j)·exp(-beta·Q(i,j))`,
>   implemented as the bounded pair `A = k0·exp(-beta·max(0,-Q))`,
>   `B = k0·exp(-beta·max(0,+Q))`. Verified to 1.4e-14, and it **deletes
>   `epochs`** — the equilibrium becomes a scalar root-find. Two costs: it puts
>   `exp` in the kernel (must route through `det_math`), and `beta` would be an
>   assembly-side inverse temperature, which the spec's new note says must not be
>   the chemical-side `Thermal`. Whether Borbax may have a second temperature is
>   Ian's call, not a literature one.
>
> - **The `K0_MAX` bound is no longer sufficient.** It was derived from fusion
>   alone; fission drains the same bins, so the bound is **joint**. Measured by
>   bisection at 400 epochs: `k0=0.25 → d0<=1.406`, `k0=0.5 → d0<=1.178`,
>   `k0=1.0 → d0<=0.970`. So `k0=1.0, d0=1.0` — inside both individually-stated
>   ranges, and `d0=1.0` is the natural top of a `0..=1` scale — goes non-finite.
>   **Worse: at `k0=0.5, d0=1.2` bins reach -0.310 while staying finite**, so
>   negativity precedes NaN and an `is_finite` guard alone misses it. Assert
>   finite **and non-negative**, over the joint draw.
>
> - **Mass-weighted argmax is the wrong statistic.** The profile is multimodal —
>   4 to 8 local maxima, inherited from `E(N)`'s shell-closure sawtooth — so the
>   argmax jumps between modes rather than sliding. Measured: `d0` 0.030 to 0.035
>   moves it **55 to 100** while mass-below-peak moves only 0.2366 to 0.2488.
>   Report mass share below/above the peak, which is smooth and monotone in `d0`.
>
> - **No committed code produces any figure in this block.** `git grep` for
>   "fission" in `*.rs` finds only doc comments; `epochs` appears in no source
>   file. Every number here came from throwaway probes. Either commit the probe
>   or mark the table unreproduced — this project's own rule is that a figure
>   without a run behind it is the stale-figure class waiting to happen.

> ## Four more measurements, two of which support the design and two of which do not
>
> - **`d0` moves universes across §7.2's boundary, continuously and unguarded.**
>   That is the exact hazard this task names for `epochs`, relocated. Measured,
>   120 seeds at 6400 epochs, universes failing `f_w > 2`: **1/120** at `d0 = 0`,
>   14/120 at 0.1, 17/120 at 0.2, **30/120 at the block's own 0.5**, and 102/120
>   at 0.75. At 200 seeds, `d0 = 0.5` gives **56/200 — 28%**.
>
>   The top-decile share *is* bounded without a ceiling constant (0.198 → 0.023
>   across the range), so that half of the claim holds. But top-decile hides a
>   new pathology: the single fullest bin's share goes **0.082 with no fission to
>   0.33–0.46 with it** — a third to a half of all matter in one cluster size.
>   Report `maxbin-share` alongside.
>
> - **The steady state is real, and the argmax is the wrong way to show it.**
>   Pushed 256x beyond where this block stopped (20 seeds, `d0 = 0.2`): the
>   argmax pins at 1600 epochs and **never moves again in any seed** out to
>   409,600 — while the continuous statistics keep moving, mass-mean rising
>   44.40 → 45.11 with per-4x increments decelerating 0.342, 0.166, 0.118, 0.093.
>   So the 1600-epoch reading sits **~2.9% below the asymptote**.
>
>   An integer bin index pins immediately and stays pinned regardless of whether a
>   steady state exists, so "argmax unchanged between 1600 and 6400" would pass
>   over a distribution still visibly moving — a green test over a live mechanism,
>   the mirror of the `sum(a)` defect this task already records. **Assert
>   convergence on a continuous statistic with a stated tolerance.**
>
> - **The long-time state is a function of `d0/k0` alone — this supports the
>   design and the block quotes the wrong evidence for it.** Measured with `k0`
>   spanning 8x at fixed ratio: argmax **identical**, mass-mean within 0.7%,
>   median `f_w` to four decimals. The time step drops out exactly.
>
>   Two qualifications: it holds only at **>= 25,600 epochs**, sixteen times
>   beyond the window the fission table was taken in (at 1600, `k0 = 0.125` gives
>   47.35 against 50.55). And `epochs` has not stopped being an answer so much as
>   stopped being *the* answer — you still have to run long enough for the ratio
>   to assert itself.
>
> - **Fission is a second mechanism, not one run both ways.** Two measurable
>   senses. Fusion is gated on `Q(M,N) > 0`; fission is **ungated**, and measured
>   over 200 seeds **27.9% of splits (max 93.4%) are pairs fusion refuses** — so
>   28% of reverse channels have no forward counterpart. And the rate laws differ
>   in kind: fusion's is a function of the *pair*, fission's of the *product
>   alone*. Gating fission to fusion's channel set costs almost nothing (argmax
>   47.03 → 50.87), which makes the asymmetry unjustified rather than a trade.
>
>   **And this block chose the reverse rate because of the outcome it avoids** —
>   "a reverse obeying detailed balance gives the distribution this design already
>   rejected". That is §3's rigged game one level up. (The Boltzmann premise
>   itself is *unverified*: a reviewer's reversible variant would not integrate
>   stably under forward Euler, 33/60 seeds non-finite. It wants an implicit
>   scheme before anyone acts on it.)
>
> ## THE MODEL: a time-dependent cascade tracked in mass, with recycling

**`epochs` is real and belongs in the design.** Composition must be a function of
how long the world has run: a young universe holds mostly light elements, an old
one has accumulated heavy ones. A static profile cannot express that, and the
"reduce it to one line" conclusion recorded below threw it out — the closed form
is the **asymptote**, not the model.

The trajectory toward it is a **linear chain in mass**, which is cheap, stable
and exactly conservative:

```
growth   mass f leaves bin N, mass f/N leaves the reservoir
         (one extra unit per N existing units),
         mass f*(1 + 1/N) arrives at N+1.          f = lambda(N) * m[N] * reservoir

decay    the exact reverse, driven by `instability(N)`:
         units return to the reservoir.
