```
lambda(N) = rate * s(D(N))     — s smooth and monotone in the step's own marginal
```

Measured over 60 seeds. `worst |mass - 1|` is over the whole run:

| decay | steps | mean argmax | mass in lightest 10 | mass above peak | `r` with peak | mass error |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **0.00** | 10 000 | **2.0** | **100%** | 0% | — | 1.3e-15 |
| 0.02 | 10 000 | 29.1 | 6.7% | 19% | 0.715 | 3.3e-15 |
| **0.10** | 10 000 | **55.4** | 5.6% | 66% | **0.849** | 6.6e-15 |
| 0.50 | 10 000 | 51.0 | 13.8% | 74% | 0.700 | 1.4e-14 |
| 0.02 | 100 000 | 56.0 | 5.1% | 73% | **0.865** | 1.4e-14 |

**Four things this settles.**

1. **Young is light, old is heavy, and it is emergent.** Mass in the lightest ten
   elements runs 100% -> 5%; mass above the peak runs 0% -> 73%. Nothing is
   fitted to produce that; it is where the front has got to.
2. **Decay FEEDS the cascade — it does not bound it.** At `decay = 0` the whole
   thing **stalls at dimers**: argmax 2.0, 100% of mass in the lightest ten.
   Growth consumes the light reservoir, and without recycling it exhausts. This
   is the third distinct role this channel has been assigned in this task and the
   first one measured: it is not "heavy clusters are least stable so fission
   removes them", and it is not "fission degrades peak-tracking" (true of the
   *pairwise* model, which had no reservoir constraint).
3. **The physics genuinely drives it.** `r` with the binding peak reaches
   **0.865**, beating the pairwise fusion kernel's 0.786 and the static
   one-liner's 0.652 — at `O(n)` per step rather than `O(n^2)`, with mass exact
   to 1.4e-14 over 100 000 steps and no stability clamp needed.
4. **Mass is conserved by construction**, because growth debits the reservoir for
   the unit it consumes. That is precisely what the retired linear-transport
   model got wrong — it shifted bins without paying, and created mass by a factor
   of `n_elements`.

### Should heavier elements decay faster? Measured: the question is the wrong shape

**In our universe the premise does not hold the way intuition suggests.** Being
less bound *per unit* and being unstable are different things: the elements below
the binding peak are overwhelmingly **stable**, not slowly-decaying. What drives
real instability is a property Borbax has no axis for — the ratio of two nucleon
species — plus, at the heavy end, electrostatic repulsion growing faster than a
short-range attraction. So "heavier decays faster" is true *above* the peak,
noisily, and simply does not apply below it. And half-lives are a property of
isotopes rather than elements, which is the same missing axis again.

**Borbax's `instability` rises toward both extremes, so it says the light end is
the most unstable — measured, the light quarter's mean is 7x the heavy end's.**
That is physically backwards for our universe. The obvious repair is to make it
**one-sided**: instability only above the peak, zero at or below it.

**Measured, that repair destroys the model.** 60 seeds, 10 000 steps:

| `instability` | decay | mean argmax | mass in lightest 10 | `r` with peak |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| symmetric (current) | 0.10 | **55.4** | 5.6% | **0.849** |
| **one-sided** | 0.02 | **2.0** | **100%** | — |
| **one-sided** | 0.10 | **2.0** | **100%** | — |
| **one-sided** | 0.50 | **2.0** | **100%** | — |

At every decay strength the cascade stalls at dimers, because the light end is
where the recycling happens. **The light-side term is load-bearing, not an
artefact.**

**So state the merge rather than defending the analogy.** Two different processes
are sharing one scalar:

- **light end** — a small, weakly-bound cluster coming apart. That is
  *dissociation*, and it is chemistry: few contacts, little binding, easily
  broken.
- **heavy end** — a cluster too large to hold together, which is the
  nuclear-side analogue.

Both return units to the reservoir, so both are the same *transition* in this
model. §8.3 says one mechanism reused beats two mechanisms bolted together, and
by that standard the merge is the right call — but it must be written down as a
deliberate merge of two processes, not left looking like a single physical claim
about decay. Otherwise the next reader repairs the light side on physical grounds
and silently deletes the recycling.

*Discriminator:* the one-sided variant above. It must **fail** — if a future
change makes one-sided instability work, the recycling has moved somewhere else
and this block is stale.

**Open, and to be measured rather than assumed:** the usable `(rate, decay)`
band and whether it needs one drawn scalar or two — note `decay = 0.1` at 10 000
steps and `decay = 0.02` at 100 000 land in the same place, so the controlling
quantity is plausibly a product, as the pairwise model's `d0/k0` was. Task 20's
battery locates it; this task must not choose it.

## Superseded: "the fix is one line"

**Kept because the measurement in it is still the right one, and because the
conclusion drawn from it was wrong in an instructive way.**

**Read this before building anything in this task.**

The process is fractional at *every* step: only some share of element 1 becomes
element 2, only some share of that becomes the next, and so on. A constant
per-step share compounds to `f^N`, i.e. **`exp(-N · ln(1/f))`** — which is
`exp(-units * decay)`, the profile V0 already ships. `element.rs` says so in its
own words: *"Sequential addition with a constant per-step survival gives
`r^N = exp(N ln r)`."*

So `decay` is not a placeholder constant. **It is the per-step conversion loss**,
and its drawn range `[0.04, 0.16]` corresponds to **85.2%–96.1% converting at
each step**. Every kernel, transport matrix, matrix power and fission channel
this task has proposed was numerically reconstructing something with a closed
form.

### What the elaborate versions actually buy, measured

The one thing a constant fraction cannot do is let the *table* shape the profile.
Making the per-step fraction a function of that step's own marginal energetics
does. Measured over 200 seeds, correlation between the mass-weighted argmax and
the binding peak (mean peak 49.48):

| model | mean argmax | r with peak | cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| **shipped** — constant fraction | 12.36 | **-0.024** | already there |
| **fraction from the step's marginal** | 30.89 | **0.652** | **one line** |
| full pairwise fusion kernel | 63.51 | 0.786 | an entire subsystem |

Two things follow, and they should decide this task.

**The shipped profile carries no information about the universe's binding peak.**
`r = -0.024`; its argmax is set by the drawn constant alone. That is the honest
justification for this task existing — not "the shape should look more like
something", but "abundance is currently independent of the chemistry it belongs
to".

**And the one-line change recovers most of the available signal.** `0.652`
against the kernel's `0.786`, for a subsystem that needs a pair iteration, a
jointly-derived stability bound, a pinned accumulation order, a drawn rate ratio,
an epoch count, and which — measured — *degrades* peak-tracking as soon as the
fission channel it also needs is switched on.

### So the task reduces to

```
abundance(N) = abundance(N-1) * s(D(N))       // s smooth and monotone in D
```

reusing `decay`'s existing draw slot for `s`'s scale, so the draw sequence does
not move (see the BLOCKER above for why that matters). No epochs, no kernel, no
stability bound, no second channel. `D` is the marginal the table already
computes, and `s` must be **smooth rather than a threshold** because `D` saws at
every shell closure.

Everything below this line — the fusion kernel, the fission channel, the
equilibrium analysis, the `d0/k0` ratio — is **retained as measured evidence for
why the simple form is the right one**, not as a specification to execute. The
one thing worth carrying forward from it is the participation constraint below,
which is orthogonal: `s(D)` decides *where* converting matter goes, a
participating fraction decides *how much* ever converts.

## The limiter is participation, not energetics — and that is one drawn scalar

**Every version of this task has tried to bound the heavy end with energetics,
and every one has failed.** The exothermicity gate bites in 4.4% of universes on
the channel that carries the mass. The fission channel degrades the very
peak-tracking it was added to explain, and shreds the light end instead of the
heavy one. In a well-mixed box run to equilibrium there is nothing to stop
arbitrary conversion, so each attempt reached for a stronger rate law and got a
different degenerate outcome.

**The constraint that is missing is not a rate. It is that most matter never
enters the process at all.** Assembly happens where conditions allow it;
conditions allow it in a small part of the system; and material that gets
furthest up the chain is the most likely not to escape the region that made it.
Both limits are about *participation and escape*, and neither is expressible as a
reaction rate.

That is one scalar, and it is the honest form of what the retired component 1 was
faking:

```
participating_fraction  — the share of matter that ever enters assembly.
                          The remainder stays feedstock, permanently.
```

It delivers "the overwhelming majority is the lightest element" as a
**consequence of a participation constraint** rather than as a fitted amplitude,
and it does so with no imported vocabulary — the retired design needed four
named amplitudes to produce the same shape. It also composes with the drawn
`d0/k0` rather than competing with it: the ratio decides *where* participating
matter settles, the fraction decides *how much* participates.

**Two things it does not do**, stated so it is not oversold like its
predecessors:

- It does not produce multi-modal structure. One participating population at one
  condition still gives one mode. Structure needs the regions in option 2.
- It is not derived. It is a drawn scalar with a physical meaning, which is the
  same standing as `decay_scale` — legitimate, and requiring Task 20's battery to
  locate its band rather than this task choosing one.

*Discriminator:* the non-participating share must be **inert in `f_w`**, exactly
as the option-1 block requires of the primordial reservoir — valence-0 mass
contributes `x·0` to both sums. If feedstock moves `f_w`, it is participating and
the parameter is not doing what it says.

## The assumption underneath every failed version: one well-mixed box

**Both retired designs, and the current one, assume a single homogeneous
population.** The four-component profile hand-fitted a shape onto one box; the
transport model ran one rate pair over one box; the fusion/fission model runs one
`d0/k0` over one box. Every failure has the same signature — *one* equilibrium,
reached everywhere at once: mass piles at the table edge, or at the peak, or
shatters to monomer, depending on a single ratio.

**A stratified system cannot be reproduced by a well-mixed one, and that is a
statement about the model rather than about anything real.** If assembly happens
in regions at different conditions, each region runs a *different* point of the
same physics, and the observed inventory is the superposition of what each
produced. A single box has no way to express that: one condition, one outcome.

Which reframes the whole task. The original four-component design was an attempt
to fake the *output* of a stratified system without modelling stratification —
that is why it needed four hand-fitted amplitudes and why removing them (the
transport rewrite) collapsed the profile to a single mode. **The components were
standing in for regions.**

So there are exactly three honest options, and the choice is a design decision,
not a measurement:

1. **Accept a single-box transient.** One region, quench-limited, controlled by
   how long it ran. Simple, defensible, and produces a unimodal profile — so
   Step 1's "not monotone" discriminator must go, along with any claim to
   reproducing structure that only stratification can make.
2. **Stratify.** Several regions at different drawn conditions, each running the
   same mechanism, composition summed. This is where multi-modal structure
   *comes from* rather than being fitted — but regions are spatial, V0 has one
   well-mixed beaker by construction (§8.6, §11), and this is therefore V1 work.
   It is also the first concrete instance of the zoom-scale note filed against
   Task 15.
3. **Drop the ambition.** V0's existing one-line profile already satisfies §7.2
   in 2000 of 2000 universes. This whole task is an enhancement.

**Do not implement option 1 while describing it as option 2.** That is what the
four-component design did, and it is why it needed amplitudes nothing derived.

## The physics argument this task should engage rather than route around
>
> In a real closed system the equilibrium cluster distribution **is** heavy-
> dominated. The reason our universe is not all iron is that assembly is
> kinetically arrested — by expansion and cooling — so abundance genuinely *is* a
> quench-time-limited transient, and the age of the world is the physically
> correct control.
>
> Which means the discomfort with `epochs` recorded above may be discomfort with
> the right answer. If abundance is a transient, `epochs` is not a fit parameter
> standing in for physics; it *is* the physics, and the thing needing a drawn
> value is the quench rate. Decide that before implementing, because it changes
> which quantity gets the `Domain::Universe` slot.

**What it buys, and each of these was an open finding:**

- **A real equilibrium.** At `d0 = 0.5` the profile is unchanged between 1600 and
  6400 epochs (argmax 22.8 both). The "front stops" assertion finally has
  something to measure that is not the table edge, and "successive differences
  shrink" becomes true rather than true-only-in-the-forbidden-regime.
- **An emergent stopping point.** The peak of the mass distribution moves toward
  the *binding* peak — 66.2 with no fission, 50.5 against a peak of 47.5 at
  `d0 = 0.2`. That is a stopping point arriving as a consequence, and it is
  what the "derived cutoff" story wanted and could not get from the exothermicity
  gate alone (which bites in only 4.4% of universes on the any-pair channel).
- **`epochs` stops being the answer.** With a steady state, the long-time limit
  is set by the fusion/fission *balance* — a ratio of two physical rates — rather
  than by how long the integrator ran. That directly addresses the recorded
  objection that `epochs` is degenerate with `k0` and is a fit parameter swept
  until §7.2 passes.
- ~~**The heavy end is bounded without a ceiling.**~~ **False — see the inverted
  mechanism above.** `instability` rises toward both extremes and `E(N)` is flat
  past the peak, so the heavy end is the part fission touches *least* (light
  quarter more unstable in 444/444 universes).

**Open, and needing measurement before Step 3:**

1. **`d0`'s bound — derived now, and it has a silent-corruption window.**
   Measured, 60 seeds at 400 epochs, `k0 = 0.5`:

   | `d0` | seeds with a negative bin | seeds non-finite |
   |---|---|---|
   | 0.9 | 0/60 | 0 |
   | **0.99** | **60/60** | **0** |
   | 1.0 | 60/60 | 0 |
   | 1.1 | 60/60 | 0 |
   | 1.25 | 60/60 | 46 |

   **There is a window `d0 ∈ [0.99, 1.1]` where every seed carries a negative
   abundance and nothing is `NaN` or `inf`.** At `d0 = 1.0` the sweep still
   reports `f_w = 1.680` and `p_c = 1.470` — plausible numbers over 60/60
   corrupted runs. So `is_finite` **cannot** be the guard; it must be
   `>= 0.0`, and the existing assertion covers only `k0`.

   And the bound is neither `d0 <= 1` nor `k0 + d0 <= 1`: `(0.5, 0.6)` is clean
   at sum 1.1 while `(0.5, 0.99)` fails at sum 1.49. It needs deriving jointly,
   the way `K0_MAX` was.
2. **Which split.** Uniform over all `(M, K-M)` was measured here. The
   alternatives — split at the midpoint (spontaneous-fission-like), or shed a
   monomer — are different physics, and Borbax's one axis admits all
   three. Pick one and say why; do not average them.
3. **Detailed balance.** Fission should plausibly reverse only pairs that fusion
   admits, or the two channels describe different reaction sets and the
   equilibrium is not the equilibrium of anything.
4. **The `> 1.0` clamp on `instability` — CLOSED: this channel inherits it, and
   the inheritance is measurably inert.** Measured over 200 seeds, elements with
   `units >= 2` (the only ones that can fission at all) whose raw deficit exceeds
   1.0 number **0.030 per universe**, present in **1 of 200**. The monomer always
   clamps and has no splits. Removing the clamp entirely changes argmax, mass-mean,
   median `f_w` and the §7.2 failure count by **nothing to four decimals**, at both
   `d0 = 0.2` and `d0 = 0.5`. The category error is real and stays Task 15's.

### The fast-forward is direct iteration, not a matrix power

`Q` depends on which two clusters meet, so the step is **bilinear**: the operator
depends on the current state. There is no time-invariant `M`, so `M^epochs` by
repeated squaring does not exist. An earlier version of this task specified
exactly that and it was wrong — and not marginally: **the squaring is only
available for the linear model, which is the one that creates mass.** Exact
conservation and `O(log epochs)` are mutually exclusive here, and conservation is
the one worth keeping.

**Where the result lives is a Step 3 requirement, not an implementation
detail.** `abundance` moves from a field read to
`abundance(universe, epochs) -> Vec<f64>`, and a call costs `O(n^2 * epochs)`.
A field read is free; a free function is not, and `abundance(&u, cfg.epochs)`
inside a loop is a natural thing to write once `epochs` arrives from beaker
config. That is the §8.6 boundary being crossed by a **signature change** rather
than by a feature, which is the disguise CLAUDE.md names. Require: computed once
at beaker construction and stored, passed onward as `&[f64]`, with the §8.6
prohibition in the function's own doc. *Discriminator:* a test asserting
`abundance` is called exactly once across N beaker steps.

It costs nothing to give up the matrix power. A fusion epoch is
`O(n_elements^2)` and the window this runs in is **tens of epochs, not
billions** (see below), so the whole
history is ~1e6 flops, once, at generation time. For scale, a *linear chain* step
is `O(n)` and a million of them measured at **146 ms** — the fast-forward was
never the expensive part. §8.6 clean: per-universe, computed once.

Deterministic, with no per-epoch RNG: the draws go into the rate constant, not
the iteration.

**Three things must be pinned, and an earlier version pinned only the smallest of
them.** It named the pair-iteration order and nothing else. Measured:

| what | effect on median `f_w` at 30 epochs |
|---|---|
| pair order (`i` ascending, then `j >= i`) | pinned — the *smallest* of the three |
| the `a == b` self-collision factor: `k0*x_a*x_b` vs `0.5*k0*x_a*x_b` | **2.3910 vs 2.2650** |
| Jacobi (accumulate into `dx`, apply after) vs Gauss-Seidel (update in place) | **2.3910 vs 2.4320**, median relative gap 1.3% |

All three are §13.1 pins. Note the figures quoted in the table below were
measured under the **half** convention and Jacobi; state that at the
implementation site, because an implementer reading "rate `k0*x_i*x_j`" writes
the full convention and gets a different chemistry.

**"Pin the pair-iteration order" is not enough on its own** — it names the
enumeration and leaves two other free choices that move the bits, while
forbidding two that do not. Measured over 40 seeds at 400 epochs:

| choice | differs bit-for-bit | free? |
|---|---|---|
| delta writes issued **at the pair** vs gathered per bin | **40/40** | no — pin it |
| `i` outer / `j >= i` inner vs `j` outer / `i <= j` inner | **40/40** | no — pin it |
| `0.5*k0*a*a` vs `(0.5*k0)*a*a` | 0/40 | **free** — 2^-1 scales exactly (0 of 69M samples disagree outside subnormals) |
| `k0*a[i]*a[j]` vs `k0*a[j]*a[i]` | 0/24 | **free** — multiplication commutes exactly |

So the pin reads: **`i` outer ascending, `j` inner from `i`, and all three delta
writes (`d[i] -= f`, `d[j] -= f`, `d[i+j+1] += f`) issued at the pair, never
gathered per bin.** Recording what is *free* matters as much as what is pinned —
an over-broad pin gets "tidied" by someone who can show it does not matter.

### The rate constant has a stability bound, and it is derived

**`k0` is drawn, and above a bound the iteration diverges to `-inf`.** One step
depletes bin `M` by `k0*x[M]*(x[M] + C)` where `C = sum(x) <= 1`, so positivity
needs `2*k0*C(0) <= 1`:

| `k0` | full kernel | half kernel |
|---|---|---|
| 0.5 | 0/60 negative — **exactly the edge** | 0/60 |
| 0.75 | **60/60 negative, worst `-inf`** | 0/60 |
| 1.5 | `-inf` | **60/60, `-inf`** |

So `K0_MAX = 0.5` for the full kernel and `1.0` for the half — a **CFL-style
stability condition, derivable, not a tuning knob**. Bound the draw by it and say
which kernel the bound belongs to. Two independent audits derived and measured
this separately and agree once the convention is stated: outflow from bin `i` is
`k0*a[i]*(a[i] + C)` with `C = sum(a) <= 1` falling monotonically, so the half
kernel is stable to `k0 <= 1.0` (measured: 0/24 seeds negative at 1.0, **24/24
negative at epoch 1 at 1.0000001**, 24/24 non-finite by epoch 13 at 1.5) and the
full kernel to 0.5. Without that, one universe in a sweep produces
`NaN`/`inf` abundances, which reach `f_w` and then the digest; and a
`f64::max`-style comparison that swallows `NaN` turns it into a plausible-looking
beaker that is silently a different universe.

**And the failure is cross-platform, not merely wrong.** Those non-finite values
arise from `inf - inf` and `inf * 0`, which `det_math.rs` records as having
*measured* architecture-dependent sign bits. `Element::abundance` is a bare
`f64`, so `canonical_bits()` does not apply and the digest hashes the raw
pattern. An out-of-range `k0` therefore produces a golden that differs between
the macOS/aarch64 and Linux/x86-64 legs — the one diagnosis the §13.4 matrix must
never give wrongly.

Measured with the bound respected: **0 occurrences of `NaN` or `-0.0`** across
3 x 64 seeds x 400 epochs at `k0 in {0.25, 0.5, 1.0}`. So hashing
`abundance.to_bits()` is safe *given* the bound, and only given it.

*Discriminator:* assert every bin is finite and non-negative after **every**
epoch, across the whole drawn `k0` range, at 400 epochs.

### Three numerics worries, measured and closed — do not re-open without new data

- **`Q`'s sign is not cancellation-sensitive, and must not get an epsilon.**
  Over **4 245 101 pairs across 2000 seeds**: zero pairs with `Q == 0.0`, zero
  below `1e-12` of the operand scale, smallest `|Q|/scale` **2.52e-8** — a
  **1.14e8-ulp** margin. Two different reassociations give identical signs on all
  4.2M pairs and an identical exothermic-pair set in 500/500 seeds. An epsilon
  here would be a physics constant admitted as a numerical tolerance, with
  nothing to calibrate it against.
- **No FMA contraction**, verified rather than assumed on the pinned rustc
  1.97.1: **0 FMA instructions** in the fusion loop on aarch64 and on
  x86-64 with `+fma,+avx2`, against a positive control where `mul_add` emits
  `fmadd`/`vfmadd213sd`. Rust's float semantics RFC makes this a language
  guarantee rather than an artefact. `Cargo.toml`'s
  `suboptimal_flops = "allow"` already stops the lint that would suggest
  `mul_add` here. The kernel calls no `libm` function, so a future `libm` cannot
  introduce one.
- **`det_math` is not needed by the kernel as specified** — the gate and the flux
  use `+ - *` and comparison only, and `energy_per_unit`'s one transcendental
  (`cbrt`) already routes through it. **The trigger to watch:** the rate function
  is still unwritten, and any Arrhenius or power-law form (`exp(-Q/T)`,
  `powf`, `ln`) must go through `det_math`. `clippy::disallowed_methods` catches
  the inherent-method spelling but not a direct `libm::exp`, which is why the
  doctrine is to add a `det_math` wrapper rather than call `libm` at the site.

### What the measurements say, including one that is a problem

Over 120 seeds, `k0 = 0.5`, abundance-weighted:

| epochs | `f_w > 2` | median `f_w` | median top-decile mass share |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0/120 | 1.0000 | 0.0000 |
| 10 | 27/120 | 1.8469 | 0.0000 |
| **30** | **120/120** | **2.2651** | **0.0000** |
| **100** | **120/120** | **3.0955** | **0.0217** |
| 400 | 120/120 | 3.4489 | 0.1370 |

**There is a real §7.2 window and it is wide** — every universe passes
Molloy-Reed from ~30 epochs on, with the heavy end still holding ~2% of mass at
100. That is a decisive improvement on the linear model, whose absorbing state
put **100%** of mass in the top decile and which failed `f_w > 2` continuously as
`epochs` rose.

**The gate bites in one channel and not the other, and a single "is any pair
exothermic" number hides that.** Measured over 500 seeds:

| channel | turns endothermic before the table edge |
|---|---|
| **symmetric** `A_N + A_N -> A_2N` | **399 of 500 — 79.8%**, median largest paying `N = 33` |
| **monomer capture** `A_N + A_1 -> A_{N+1}` | **25 of 500 — 5.0%** |

An earlier version of this block reported "4.4%" and concluded there is "no
derived cutoff at all". That figure was the largest cluster reachable by *any*
exothermic pair, which the monomer channel dominates, so it described the weakest
channel and attributed the result to the whole model.

**The distinction follows from the arithmetic, and needs no analogy to state.**
For a symmetric pair, `Q(N, N) = 2N * (E(2N) - E(N))`, so symmetric fusion is
exothermic **iff `E(2N) > E(N)`** — which fails as soon as `2N` is past the
binding peak. That predicts a symmetric stopping point near *half* the peak, and
the measurement agrees: `N = 33` against a median `peak` of 55.

For the monomer channel, `Q(N, 1) = D(N) - E(1)`, the marginal, which stays
positive far longer because `E(N)` falls only slowly past the peak. Two different
conditions on the same table, so two different frontiers. Nothing about our
universe is needed to derive that, and **an earlier version of this paragraph
derived it from stellar nucleosynthesis instead** — naming a real element, a real
capture reaction and a real barrier, and asserting Borbax "reproduces exactly
that split". That is precisely the Borbax↔real mapping §5's G3 forbids, in a
block this plan says becomes code comments, written in a task whose own
resolution block claims the nucleosynthesis literature was not consulted. Task
4's rule names the failure directly: not "magic numbers", not "a noble family",
not "an iron-peak analogue".

*Consequence for this task:* the model must be **channel-aware**, or it will
report the monomer channel's behaviour as the universe's. Do not collapse the
gate to one scalar.

**What is still true and still a problem:** the monomer channel almost never
walls, so mass does climb to the table edge given enough epochs. The cause is not
the monomer's own energy — a hypothesis that `E(1) = -sigma < 0` (a lone atom is
charged strain with no contacts to offset it) was making capture always
favourable was **measured and refuted**: forcing `E(1) = 0` moves the figure from
5.0% to 5.4%.

The cause is that `energy_per_unit` is too **flat** past the peak. On seed 0 it
falls from 4.4404 at `peak = 60` to 4.2958 at `N = 95` — a 3% decline across 37%
of the table — while `N*E(N)` rises from 266 to 408. For the marginal to go
negative you need `E'(N) < -E(N)/N`, a relative decline steeper than `1/N`, and
the measured curve is nowhere near it. That is the strain term `sigma * N^(2/3)`
— already recorded as chosen rather than derived, already measured to move the
peak in 48.1% of drawn universes — and this is a second, independent measurement
pointing at it. Attach it to that open item (Task 20) rather than working around
it here.

Do not paper over this. It says something specific and useful: Borbax's
`energy_per_unit` does not fall fast enough past `peak` for pairwise fusion to
turn endothermic, and the term that governs how fast it falls is the strain
`sigma * N^(2/3)` — **already recorded as chosen rather than derived**, and
already measured to move the peak in 48.1% of drawn universes. This is a second,
independent measurement pointing at the same term, and it should be attached to
that open item (Task 20) rather than worked around here.

Until it is fixed, state plainly what this task delivers: **an assembly whose
symmetric channel is energy-limited in ~80% of universes and whose monomer
channel is time-limited in ~95%.** Neither half of that is the sentence "the
cutoff is derived from the table", and the plan must not compress it back into
one.

### Where `epochs` lives

The universe generates `M` (Layer 0, immutable, §7). `epochs` is **not** a
universe constant — it is the age of the world being simulated, so it belongs
with world-gen (§11, V1), which is exactly where §11 already puts "element
abundances".

For V0, which has no world: one scalar with a stated default, threaded from the
beaker's configuration — and therefore **inside `config_hash`** in §13.1's
`(universe_seed, world_seed, config_hash)` identity tuple. `epochs` changes every
abundance in the universe, so a run that omits it from the tuple reports two
different chemistries under one identity. Named here because `borbax-cli` and the
config type do not exist yet, so this must land *with* the beaker rather than be
discovered from a divergent golden. Do **not** put it back on `Element` to avoid plumbing it
— that is the mistake this task is undoing.

*This also answers a §7.1 question left open:* whether `abundance`'s direction
should be drawn like `affinity`'s. It should not, and it is not an exception to
§7.1's rule — it is out of its scope. §7.1 distinguishes **invented free
scalars** (draw the sign) from **consequences of a construction** (`mass`
"increases down the table with noise", and nobody proposes drawing that sign).
Under transport, abundance is squarely the second kind.

**The boundary between 2 and 3 must be derived, and `peak` is NOT it.**

`peak` is `argmax E(N)` where `E` is binding energy *per unit*. That is the
criterion for **symmetric** assembly — two comparable clusters fusing — because
`Q(N, N) = 2N * (E(2N) - E(N))`, which changes sign exactly where `E` does. (An
earlier version justified this by naming the element it corresponds to in our
universe. It does not need the analogy, and Task 4's rule forbids it.) But
`abundance`'s own comment
describes **sequential** addition ("each extra unit costs a step"), for which
"still paying" is the marginal `D(N) = N*E(N) - (N-1)*E(N-1) > 0`.

They are not close. Measured over `k` x `eps` x `sigma` x `n` = 3888 cells:

| | |
|---|---|
| `peak` equals the last still-exothermic N | **14.2%** |
| `peak` is *earlier* than it | **85.8%** |
| assembly still exothermic at the table edge (no cutoff in range) | **57.6%** |
| median gap `last_pay - peak` | **40 elements, 41.7% of the table** |

So using `peak` would label 41.7% of the table (median) as "past the point
assembly can reach" while the universe's own energetics says otherwise.

**Decide the assembly process first, then derive the cutoff from that process.**
If symmetric, keep `peak` and rewrite `element.rs`'s "sequential addition" story,
because a profile built from `exp(-r*N)` components is a sequential shape and
would otherwise contradict its own cutoff.

**If sequential — and it is; `contacts_upto` adds one unit at a time and
`element.rs` says "each extra unit costs a step" — the cutoff is the `N` where
assembly FIRST stops paying, not the largest `N` that pays.** This block said
"the largest `N` with `D(N) > 0`" and that is a different quantity, because `D`
is a **sawtooth**: total binding energy is `eps*c(N) - sigma*N^(5/3)` and
`c(N) - c(N-1)` jumps at shell closures, so `D` inherits the jumps. Measured over
2000 drawn seeds: `D` changes sign a median of **0** times and up to **7**;
`first_stop - 1 == last_pay` in only **0.35%**; where both exist the median gap
is **40 elements**. A sequential process halts at the first step that does not
pay — it cannot jump a barrier and resume. Having argued "derive the cutoff from
the process", the block then supplied a rule contradicting the process it chose,
by a median of 40 elements.

*Discriminator:* assert `first_stop` and `last_pay` differ (they agree in 0.35%
of seeds), and name which one the implementation uses **in the same sentence as
the process name**.

*Discriminator:* assert the cutoff and `peak` are **not** always equal across
seeds — measured, they differ in 85.8% of grid cells. An implementation that
makes them identical used `peak`.

Everything else about the derived-boundary principle stands: draw the amplitudes
and rates, derive every boundary.

## RESOLVED — how the fiction objection was raised and what closed it

**Recorded because the objection was correct and the resolution is better than
the design it replaced, which is the outcome a precedence-1 finding should aim
for rather than a veto.**

An earlier version of this task built the profile as four summed components
labelled primordial, stellar fusion, supernova and neutron-star merger, "in the
shape a real universe's element inventory is built", with the stated limit
"fidelity is shape-only — no real abundance ratios".

That limit protects G1 and G5 correctly: no real data enters, every amplitude is
drawn. **The objection was that G3 is about structure, not numbers** — §7.1
already draws that line for period lengths, where a fixed candidate list *is*
real shell structure "however randomly it is sampled".

The checkable form, which is the one that mattered: **Borbax has no cosmology at
any layer.** §7 is Layer 0 generated physics; §11's world-gen is terrain, climate
and spatial abundances. There is no star, no supernova and no merger anywhere in
the spec. So three of the four components were labels on curves for events the
code never computes — the same move `packing.rs` refuses by name about
`10n^2+2`. `PhysicsVersion`'s doc already supplies the test: a law must "state
the physical process it adds in terms that name no biological structure",
because naming its own outcome is the failure. "Supernova" has no referent in
Borbax to be restated in terms of.

**What closed it:** running Borbax's own assembly energetics forward over epochs,
above. Every rate is a function of the table; the cutoff is not a parameter; the
heavy tail is what leaks past a barrier given time rather than a term added to
produce it; and the input is one scalar with a physical meaning Borbax *does*
have — how long the world has been running, in `WorldYear`.

**What was explicitly not done:** the nucleosynthesis literature was not
consulted to decide which process Borbax models, and no real abundance
structure, ratio or process data was imported. Deferring to it is the
isomorphism G3 forbids. The question restates as *which assembly process does
Borbax's own energetics describe?* — and the code answers it: `contacts_upto`
uses `remaining = units - 1`, one unit at a time, and `element.rs` says "each
extra unit costs a step". Borbax's assembly is sequential because that is what
the code does.

**One fact that bounds how much any of this is worth.** Abundance does not enter
§9.3's RAF detection or §9.6's death definition *at all* — RAF's food set `F` is
a set of molecule *types*, carrying no quantities or concentrations, and the
algorithm tests membership. Abundance enters exactly two places: §7.2's `f_w`,
and V0's initial conditions in a closed beaker. So the entire justification
budget is paid out of §7.2 — which is where the option-1 finding below shows a
mis-sited reservoir does real damage.

## The recorded rejection this must not reproduce, and why it does not

`abundance`'s own doc records that a draft carried an `exp(beta *
energy_per_unit)` tilt at `beta = 0.6`, and that it was killed on measurement:
*"implementing the stated story makes the heaviest element the most abundant and
collapses `p_c` to 0.112"*. That is a different mechanism — a Boltzmann tilt
toward binding energy — and this is a transport process in which the initial
condition sits entirely at `units == 1` and everything above it is reached only
by paying for each step, so the light end dominates unless `epochs` is run far
enough to empty it. **The failure mode is avoided by construction, and the test
that caught it must be re-run anyway**, because `abundance` is §7.2's gel lever
and CLAUDE.md names the decay band as the first thing to suspect when a run
produces nothing.

## What this interacts with

Task 5's review measured that the bond matrix is exactly rank 1 and its single
ranking is **98.86% concordant with atom count** — "bigger is stronger", where
the previous derivation had an interior peak. The standing argument that this is
harmless is *"large elements are rare anyway, because abundance falls
geometrically"*. **Running the epoch fast-forward far enough weakens exactly
that argument** — mass climbing the table is the whole mechanism — so this task
must re-measure it across the epoch range rather than inherit the conclusion.

## What this task does NOT produce, and where the rest goes

**This task produces an inventory of *elements*. The beaker does not start with
bare atoms.**

A dust cloud already contains water and organic molecules; planet formation and
cooling drive a great deal of chemistry through heat alone. So by the time
anything in V0 is being simulated, a substantial **pre-generated molecular
inventory** already exists — and that, not a jar of loose atoms, is the beaker's
correct initial condition.

That inventory is deliberately **out of scope here** and must not be smuggled in
by making `abundance` a distribution over molecules. Reasons, in order:

1. Molecules need `borbax-molecule`, which does not exist until Task 6, and
   species identity needs canonicalisation and interning. Layer 0 cannot depend
   on Layer 1.
2. §7.2's `f_w` is a statement about *atoms* and their valences. Mixing molecular
   species into it would change the population the gel criterion reads without
   changing the criterion.

*Routed:* the pre-generated molecular inventory is the **beaker's initial
condition**, and belongs with whichever task first fills a beaker. It should be
produced the same way everything else here is — by running the chemistry, at a
temperature, from the element inventory this task computes — and not by a
separate authored list, which would be a second mechanism (§8.3) and a fiction
hazard besides.

*One consequence that lands on already-written code:* `UniverseConsts::solvent`
is an `ElementId`. A universal solvent is far more plausibly a **molecule** than
a bare element — that is what "whatever accounts for the universe's universal
solvent" being part of the pre-generated inventory implies. Changing it means
`solvent` becomes a `SpeciesId`, which cannot exist before Task 6. Flagged here
rather than in a doc comment so the decision is made deliberately when Task 6
lands, not discovered when §9.5's solvent attack is written.

## Decided: do NOT recount the shells. The inert monomer stays.

**A first version of this section said the monomer is inert "because of where the
shell loop starts counting, not because of anything geometric", and proposed
recounting so it gets valence 1 like hydrogen. That was wrong, and the way it was
wrong is the one this project records most often.**

`filled = 1` is not an artefact of an initial condition. It is the *same*
physical model `contacts_upto` uses — `packing.rs:377` is `remaining = units - 1`
with `prev_cap = 1`, i.e. one core plus shells — and the two are correct only
together. Measured over the full drawn grid (`k` 6..=14 x `eps` 9 x `sigma` 12):

| closure convention | `energy_per_unit_is_locally_maximal_at_every_closure` |
|---|---|
| current (`filled = 1`) | **0** non-maximal of 2268 |
| recount (`filled = 0`) | **2268** non-maximal of 2268 |

Under the recount, `N = 12` — an icosahedral shell with an *empty centre* —
becomes the closed inert element, and `N = 13`, the actual complete Mackay
cluster, gets valence 1. §7.1's periodicity story inverts by one unit while every
individual number stays plausible.

**And the derivation claimed for valence 1 does not hold.** The prose argument was
"a lone sphere has its entire surface available to bond" — run through this
module's own model that gives the free capacity as `z = k + 2`, which is 8 to 16,
and it is exactly what `contacts_upto` computes for the core. Valence 1 arrives
from somewhere else: `frontier_notches(k+2, 1)` lands in `[1.1429, 1.3333]` for
every `k` and rounds to 1. So the stated mechanism and the actual mechanism point
at different numbers, and the one that ships is hydrogen's.

That is `generate_elements`'s own recorded defect with a different variable — a
convention chosen because it produces a target value, then reported as a
derivation. It is also what `packing.rs:36` forbids by name about `10n^2 + 2`:
*"This is not corroboration and must never be read as any."* That comment is the
project refusing to act on a real-world resemblance. This section was acting on
one.

**The honest statement about the monomer's valence.** `frontier_notches` measures
the lateral frontier of a *partly-filled outer shell*. The monomer has no outer
shell, so the function has no defined value there and any answer is a convention.
The inert monomer is therefore not a bug — and it is *structurally unlike*
hydrogen, which is what G3 asks for.

**So: take option 1 — leave the primordial concentration on `units == 1`.**

**This reverses an earlier "take option 2" and the measurement is not close.**
Option 2 said: put it on the lightest *bondable* element instead, "the first
thing assembly makes that can react". Measured over 2000 drawn seeds, the
lightest bondable element has `valence == 1` in **2000 of 2000** — there is no
seed variation at all. It is a chain *terminator*.

And §7.2's controlling quantity is `f_w = <f^2>/<f>`, in which the two candidates
behave in opposite ways:

- **valence-0 mass is exactly neutral** — it adds `x * 0` to the numerator and
  `x * 0` to the denominator, so `f_w`, and hence `p_c`, are *identically*
  unchanged however much of it there is;
- **valence-1 mass drags `f_w` toward 1** — it adds `x` to both sums.

So option 2 swapped an `f_w`-neutral reservoir for an `f_w`-poisonous one.
Measured, piling 100x the current total abundance onto each candidate over 2000
seeds:

| where the primordial mass goes | median `f_w` | `f_w <= 2` (no giant component at any extent) |
|---|---|---|
| **option 1 — `units == 1`, inert** | **2.4428** | **0 of 2000** |
| option 2 — lightest bondable, valence 1 | 1.0270 | **2000 of 2000** |

Option 1's 2.4428 *is* the untouched baseline, by the neutrality above, and it
sits beside §7.2's own reference distribution (`f_w` 2.36, `p_c` 0.734). Option 2
puts every universe below Molloy–Reed's existence criterion `f_w > 2`, so **no
giant component exists at any extent in any universe** — §7.2's polymer floor
(chains of length >= 20 forming and persisting) becomes unreachable, while the
gel *ceiling* reports a clean pass. That is precisely the failure §7.2's opening
sentence names, passing the test meant to catch it.

**The inert monomer is not a wart to route around — it is what makes an
overwhelming primordial reservoir safe.** A withdrawn earlier finding tried to
make `units == 1` bondable; this is the second, independent reason that was
wrong.

*Discriminator:* assert abundance-weighted `f_w > 2` and `p_c <= 1` in **every**
drawn universe. And report the share held by `valence <= 1`, not `valence == 0` —
measured today over 2000 seeds: inert 12.40%, terminator 14.36%, **non-extending
26.76%**. Watching only the inert half watches the half that cannot hurt `f_w`.

*Discriminator, and it does not exist today:* the element loop and the contact
recursion must be pinned as one decision. Add a test asserting that every
`closures(k, n)` entry is `outer == 0` in the shell walk **and** completes a
shell in `contacts_upto`. Nothing currently says they agree, and a change to
either alone moves every closure off its own binding maximum.

*Second, and it is why the above went unnoticed:* the shell walk is spelled
**five times** — `element.rs:456` in production and four more in tests, each with
`(0, 1)` hardcoded. Two of those tests would have gone green and wrong under the
recount, including the affinity range assertion whose own comment says it "fails
loudly the moment it moves" (measured: lo goes 0.140476 to 0.147619 and the
assertion still passes). Extract one `shell_walk(k, units)` and route all five
through it, on the argument `frontier_notches` already won in this codebase.

## The consequence, and why option 1 does not have it

The worry that produced option 2 was: inert elements hold a share of total
abundance, a primordial concentration raises it, and §7.2's gel lever counts
bonds per node — nodes that cannot bond are not a small correction to `p_c`, they
are a different graph.

**The premise is right and the conclusion is exactly backwards.** Because
valence-0 mass contributes `0` to both the numerator and the denominator of
`f_w`, a larger inert share is not a different graph at all — it is the *same*
graph in a larger beaker. The reactive subnetwork's `f_w`, and therefore `p_c`,
are bit-identical. What a large inert share actually costs is **dilution**: `p_ss`
falls because the reactive species are further apart, which moves the operating
point *away* from gel. That is a real effect, it is the one §7.2 measures via
`p_ss / p_c`, and it is bounded rather than catastrophic.

Contrast a large *terminator* share, which changes `f_w` itself and so moves the
threshold the beaker is being compared against.

*Discriminator:* assert the share held by `valence <= 1`, and assert that varying
the primordial amplitude moves `p_ss` while leaving `f_w` invariant to within
float noise. If `f_w` moves when only `units == 1` abundance changed, the
weighting is wrong — that invariance is a property of the arithmetic, not a
tolerance.

*Stale figure retired:* this block previously said inert elements hold "a mean
**12.05%**". No population reproduces that — measured 11.48% (seeds 0..24),
12.44% (0..64), 12.27% (0..500), **12.40% (0..2000)**, 12.43% (0..20000). Quote
the population with the number, per this repo's recorded stale-figure class.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing tests**

**The obvious three discriminators are jointly satisfied by an impostor**, so
they are not enough on their own. This passes all of them:

```rust
let abundance = det_math::exp(-(units as f64) * decay)               // unchanged
    + rp * det_math::exp(-((n_elements - units) as f64) * rp_rate)   // bump at the top
    + if units <= cutoff { pedestal } else { 0.0 };                  // step at the cutoff
```

Non-monotone: yes. Slope changes at the cutoff: yes. Moving the cutoff moves it:
yes — the cutoff is literally the branch condition. And its bulk shape is
byte-for-byte identical to the profile this task exists to replace, with no
transport in it anywhere: nothing notices. (This impostor was written against
the retired four-component design and is kept because the *lesson* is
unchanged — a piecewise curve satisfies shape assertions without carrying a
mechanism. Under transport it is harder to write, which is an argument for the
redesign rather than a reason to drop the guard.)

**The property that separates a mechanism from a shaped curve is that the
component *rates* are functions of the table.** Not their amplitudes, and not
disjointness.

> **The disjointness rule was backwards, with a constructive counterexample.**
> This block used to require that "each drawn amplitude must move a *predicted,
> disjoint* region and nothing else", on the reasoning that a fitted curve's
> knobs interact everywhere. Measured, building both and doubling each amplitude
> in turn:
>
> | component doubled | genuine superposition | windowed impostor (4 disjoint windows, all rates hardcoded) |
> |---|---|---|
> | 1 primordial | 25% of table | 2% |
> | 2 stellar | **100%** | 35% |
> | 3 post-cutoff | **100%** | 35% |
> | 4 heavy end | 74% | 10% |
>
> Disjointness is a property of the **parameterisation**, not of provenance. A
> sum of exponentials — which is what a superposition of processes *is* — has
> global support in every component and **fails** the rule; a piecewise curve
> with four typed-in rates **passes** it perfectly. The rule pushed the
> implementation toward the impostor it was written to exclude. Same shape as a
> gameable metric one level down: a criterion satisfiable by construction is not
> evidence.

> **Under transport, most of this is unrepresentable rather than tested for.**
> There are no component amplitudes to perturb independently, because there are
> no components — so the impostor above cannot be written down. What survives,
> and what the discriminators below must still carry, is the part that was never
> about components:

So assert, in addition to the three below:

- **The profile moves when the table's energetics move.** Perturb `eps`/`sigma`
  alone and the whole vector must change, at every `epochs`. Under transport this
  is nearly free — the rates *are* `D(N)` — which is the point: the property that
  needed three careful assertions in a fitted design is structural in this one.
- **Mass is conserved, exactly — `sum(N*a(N))`, not `sum(a)`.** The distinction
  is the whole assertion. An earlier version asserted `sum(a)`, which is *cluster
  count*: conserved by **every** column-stochastic matrix, so it could not fail
  for any implementation of the class being specified, while the actual mass grew
  by a factor of `n_elements`. A green test over a dead mechanism, on the one
  invariant this task singles out as not-hygiene. Under pairwise fusion the
  correct quantity is conserved by construction — measured 1.0000000000 in 40 of
  40 seeds at 400 epochs — and cluster count *falls*, which is what fusion means.
- **`epochs = 0` is the initial condition; state the band the other assertions
  hold on.** At `epochs = 0` everything is monomer, so `f_w = 0/0` is **NaN** and
  at `epochs = 1` the median is exactly 1.0000 — both below Molloy-Reed. So
  "`epochs = 0` is the initial condition" and "`f_w > 2` across the epoch range"
  **cannot both hold**, and an executor hits that on the first test. This is the
  same jointly-unsatisfiable-pair defect the block two lists above already
  records; it reproduced within one round. Bound the §7.2 assertion to a stated
  band and state it: measured, `f_w > 2` in **120/120 from ~30 epochs on**.
- **Convergence, stated as what it is.** Do *not* assert "successive differences
  shrink" as a general property: under fusion the profile is a travelling front
  and differences shrink only after it reaches the gate or the table edge —
  measured on the predecessor model, that assertion held in **0 of 300** seeds in
  the operating regime and passed only in the regime §7.2 forbids. Assert instead
  that the front *stops*: the reachable maximum is bounded by the energy gate or
  the table edge, whichever binds first.
- **§7.2 must hold at every epoch count, not at one.** Sweeping `epochs` sweeps
  the whole abundance distribution, so it sweeps `f_w`. Assert abundance-weighted
  `f_w > 2` and `p_c <= 1` across the epoch range as well as across seeds. This
  is the strongest test in the task: the option-1 finding below shows a
  mis-sited reservoir puts every universe below Molloy-Reed, and `epochs` is now
  a lever that can do the same thing continuously.
- **Report vacuity — of the quantity actually adopted.** This said "`peak ==
  n_elements` in **10.6%**", which is the vacuity of `peak`, the quantity the
  Mechanism block above *rejects*. The adopted marginal cutoff is far more often
  absent: measured over 2000 drawn seeds (not a grid), assembly is still
  exothermic at the table edge — no cutoff, **component 3 empty** — in
  **93.2%**. `peak == n_elements` reproduces at 10.2% on the same sample. So the
  "post-cutoff slope test has nothing to measure" caveat applies to **93% of
  universes, not 11%**, which is the difference between a caveat and the ordinary
  case.

  **Under transport this stops being a vacuity problem and becomes a
  measurement.** There is no cutoff *parameter* to be absent — in the 93% where
  assembly still pays at the table edge, the step rate simply never collapses and
  mass keeps climbing until it runs out of table. That is a real prediction and it
  has a real consequence worth asserting: those universes should show mass
  piling up at the **top** of the table, which is the gel direction. Report the
  census as "universes with no interior barrier", and gate those on `p_c` rather
  than skipping the test.

  *Also flagged, unresolved:* this block's grid figure "assembly still exothermic
  at the table edge — **57.6%**" does not reproduce; the same 3888-cell grid
  gives **92.1%**, while every other figure in that table reproduces to the
  digit. 57.6% is close to the complement of "a first `D <= 0` exists" (43.5% on
  drawn seeds), so it was probably measured under a different definition.
  Re-derive it before relying on it.

The three below, none of which needs a fitted constant:

1. **The profile is not monotone.** The current one is strictly decreasing in
   `units`. Under transport a **pile-up below the barrier** makes that false —
   mass arrives faster than it can proceed — so `assert!` that some `units` has
   higher abundance than a lighter neighbour, over drawn seeds and at more than
   one `epochs`. A smooth decline everywhere means either the barrier is not
   biting or the rates do not depend on `D` at all. Note this is now an
   *emergent* property with no term added to produce it, so it is a real test
   rather than a check that a component was included.
2. **The slope changes at the derived cutoff.** The cutoff must be *visible* in
   the profile — the decline past it must be steeper (or the amplitude lower)
   than before it. This is what ties the profile to a derived quantity rather
   than to a chosen index.
3. **Moving the cutoff moves the slope change.** Perturb `eps`/`sigma` so the
   binding series turns over elsewhere; the profile's inflection must follow.
   This is the discriminator that separates "shaped by the table" from "shaped
   like the table" — a profile with a hardcoded inflection passes 1 and 2 and
   fails this.

   > **These two said `peak` and had to say `cutoff`.** The Mechanism block above
   > states "the boundary between 2 and 3 must be derived, and `peak` is NOT it"
   > and requires a test asserting the cutoff and `peak` are **not** always
   > equal — while these required the inflection to sit *at* `peak`. **No
   > implementation satisfies both**, and an executor hits it on the first test
   > written. (They are jointly satisfiable only degenerately: `peak == last_pay`
   > occurs only when `peak` is at the table edge, 553 of 553 equality cases on
   > the grid.) The intent of 3 was always the right one and survives the
   > substitution verbatim, because perturbing `eps`/`sigma` is exactly what
   > distinguishes a derived inflection from a typed-in one.

Plus the regression the rejection above demands: **the heaviest element must not
be the most abundant**, over every drawn seed.

> **That regression fires never, and it is guarding the wrong half of the
> rejection — and under transport, `epochs` is the knob it has to watch.** The
> Boltzmann tilt was rejected for two reasons — argmax at the top *and* `p_c`
> collapsing to 0.112 — and only the first was carried forward. The table below
> was measured against the retired four-component design's heavy amplitude; read
> `a_heavy` as a proxy for **how far the epoch fast-forward has been run**, since
> both move mass toward the top of the table and the `p_c` consequence is the
> same.
> Measured over 256 drawn seeds with the four-component profile, sweeping the
> heavy-end amplitude against primordial = 1.0:
>
> | `a_heavy` | operating point median | top-decile abundance share | heaviest is argmax |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | 0 | 0.160 | 0.02% | 0/256 |
> | 1e-2 | 0.169 | 1.60% | 0/256 |
> | 1e-1 | 0.247 | **13.1%** | 0/256 |
> | 1e0 | **0.632** | **56.7%** | **0/256** |
>
> At `a_heavy = 1.0` the top decile holds **57% of all matter** and the argmax
> guard still reports clean. The reason it cannot fire is that a heavy-end *bump*
> raises a broad tail without ever putting the single heaviest element on top —
> so the guard tests a shape nothing here produces. Meanwhile `p_c`, the half
> that was dropped, does move: 0.519 at `a_heavy = 0.1` and **0.318** at 1.0,
> with `f_w = 4.14`. That is the sludge direction.
>
> **So components 1 and 4 push `p_c` past *opposite* §7.2 failure boundaries** —
> component 1 above 1 (no polymers at all, see the option-1 block above),
> component 4 toward gel — and the design bounds neither.
>
> **And `d0` is a tuning knob as written — three specific reasons, not a general
> suspicion.** It has **no draw anywhere**: `k0` gets an RNG slot, `d0` appears
> only as typed-in values in a measurement table, where §22.6's decay band — the
> precedent claimed for it — is a *drawn* parameter whose band was located
> empirically. The **sweep target is the test**: `d0` moves §7.2's pass rate from
> 119/120 to 90/120 across the quoted range. And it is **on the wrong side of the
> ratio**: the controlling quantity is `d0/k0`, measured, and the design draws
> `k0` — which provably drops out — while leaving `d0`, which does not, undrawn.
>
> *The turn that makes it legitimate is small:* draw `d0/k0` from
> `Domain::Universe`, bound it by the jointly-derived stability condition, and let
> Task 20's battery **locate** the band rather than choosing it here. At that
> point it is §22.6's decay band exactly.
>
> *Replace the argmax regression with:* a band on abundance-weighted `p_c`, plus
> the top-decile abundance share reported alongside — asserted **across the epoch
> range**, per the §7.2 discriminator above. The retired design needed a stated
> bound on a free amplitude (`a_heavy <= 1e-2` kept the operating point at 0.169
> and the top decile at 1.6%; 1e-1 failed). Transport needs no such constant: the
> equivalent statement is that `epochs` has a usable range, and finding it is a
> **measurement** — the same shape as §22.6's decay band, and it belongs in Task
> 20's sweep rather than being chosen here.
>
> That is the substantive gain from the redesign, not just a vocabulary one: the
> quantity that had to be bounded by fiat became a quantity the battery locates.

  > the redesign; the one that says *what to build* had not, so an executor
  > following the checkboxes would have built the four-component profile the
  > redesign exists to delete — the design rejected on precedence-1 grounds. One
  > line, and it would have undone the whole task.

- [ ] **Step 4: Re-measure the gel lever, against a stated band** — §7.2's
  `p_c`, `f_w` and bonds-per-node at gel, and the abundance-weighted decay
  figures. **"Re-measure" is not a gate: any value satisfies it.** State the band
  and the direction before implementing, from the pre-change measurement.

  Two specific hazards, measured on a plausible instantiation. The
  abundance-weighted mean `instability` moves by **0.93x-2.68x** — `instability` is
  0 at the peak and rises toward *both* extremes, which is exactly where this
  profile concentrates matter. And its cross-universe *spread* collapses, from
  [0.22, 0.66] to [0.51, 0.67]: universes become more alike in the one quantity
  §9.4's differential persistence depends on. CLAUDE.md calls the decay band the
  most sensitive parameter in the system.

  Also bound the **dynamic range**: `max/min` abundance goes from a median 5.38e3
  (2000 seeds; this read **7.3e3**, which is the *mean of log10* — 3.8869 —
  reported as a median, where the true median log10 is 3.7306)
  to a median 1.7e5, worst 2.6e12. A 120-element table with a 1e12 spread is
  eight elements of chemistry and 112 species numerically present and physically
  absent.

  **Do not choose a ceiling — derive a floor from the particle count.** V0
  simulates one small volume, not a universe: the beaker holds some large but
  *finite* number of particles (order 1e15, not 1e30 — the observable universe's
  inventory is not what is being modelled and its scale should never appear in
  this code). Any species whose abundance falls below `1 / n_particles` is not
  rare, it is **absent** — there is not one atom of it in the volume. That gives a
  physical, derived cutoff where the previous sentence asked for a chosen one, and
  it disposes of the 1e12-spread problem without a constant: species below the
  floor are simply not in the beaker.

  *Discriminator:* assert that the number of species surviving the floor is
  reported, and that it responds to `n_particles`. A model where every table entry
  is present at any particle count has not applied the floor. Note this also
  interacts with §7.2 — species below the floor must be excluded from `f_w`,
  because a valence they contribute is a valence no atom has.

  Then grep the workspace for `abundance` and for "falls geometrically" and
  requote or retract each hit — `lib.rs`'s solvent draw currently reasons from
  the geometric decay this task removes.

## Phase 3 — Molecules and shape

This is where the conceptual weight of the project sits. Every file here is
small on purpose: this is where most iteration will happen.

### Task 6: Molecule graphs and canonical labelling

**Requirement routed from Task 5, to the task that owns `add_bond` — an earlier
version named Task 11, which owns `Polymer::push` and does not mention this.**

`BondEnergyMatrix::energy` will price any order in `1..=BondOrder::MAX` for any
pair, including orders the pair cannot physically support. The real bound is
**`min(valence_a, valence_b)`**: a bond of order *n* consumes *n* slots at each
end. Nothing in `borbax-universe` can enforce it — that crate has no molecule and
`BondEnergyMatrix` deliberately holds no element data — so it lands here, at the
point a bond is actually formed.

Measured over 200 universes: the per-universe ceiling of `min(valence_a,
valence_b)` takes the values `{4, 5, 6}` and is **never 3**, and 25.6% of
bondable pairs support order 4 or more. So a type-level cap cannot be the guard —
`BondOrder::MAX` is a max over *all* universes and sits above the local ceiling
in 78% of the drawn grid.

*Discriminator:* `Mol12::add_bond` must reject an order exceeding
`min(valence_a, valence_b)` for the two elements involved, and a test must
construct one and see it refused. "The fixture happens to avoid them" does not
satisfy it, and neither does clamping — an over-cap order is a caller bug.


**Files:**
- Create: `crates/borbax-molecule/Cargo.toml`, `crates/borbax-molecule/src/lib.rs`
- Create: `crates/borbax-molecule/src/graph.rs`, `crates/borbax-molecule/src/canonical.rs`
- Test: both source files

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: `borbax_universe::{Universe, ElementId}`, `borbax_units::Mass`
- Produces: `MAX_ATOMS`, `Mol12`, `Mol12::{add_atom, add_bond, degree, mass, is_connected}`, `CanonForm`, `canonicalise(&Mol12) -> CanonForm`, `SearchStats`

**Task 5 changed four things every snippet below still spells the old way.**
Reconcile against `borbax-universe` before executing, exactly as Task 5 had to
reconcile against Task 4's rewrite. These are the *intended* effects of the three
decisions Task 4's review routed into Task 5, not accidents — decision 2 required
the `&Element` lookup to stop existing, and decision 3 required `ElementId`'s
field to close.

| Was | Is now | Why |
|---|---|---|
| `ElementId(3)` from another crate | `ElementId::from_index(3)` → `Option` | the field is `pub(crate)`; `elements[id.0 as usize]` was a panic site per call site |
| `u.elements`, `u.shell` | `u.table` — `PeriodicTable` with `get`/`iter`/`len` | names the tuple `generate_elements` returned, and closes the same panic site |
| `u.element(id).mass` | `u.element(id)` → `Option<&Element>` | clamping answered with a *different element's* properties |
| `u.bonds.energy(&a, &b, 1)` | `u.bonds.energy(id_a, id_b, BondOrder::SINGLE)` — and `BondOrder` is a validated newtype over `1..=BondOrder::MAX` (6), not a three-variant enum; build one with `BondOrder::new`/`try_from`, iterate `BondOrder::ALL` | a rate path holds ids, not 112-byte structs; `0` had no honest meaning as an order |

`Element::energy_per_unit` is `Quanta`, not `f64` (§5 G4). Anything summing or
comparing it keeps working; anything that fed it to a bare-`f64` API needs
`.get()`, and should ask first whether that API ought to take `Quanta`.

```rust
#[cfg(test)]
mod tests {
    use super::*;
    use borbax_universe::{ElementId, Universe};

    fn chain(n: u8) -> Mol12 {
        let mut m = Mol12::new();
        for i in 0..n {
            m.add_atom(ElementId(i % 4));
        }
        for i in 0..n.saturating_sub(1) {
            m.add_bond(i, i + 1, 1);
        }
        m
    }

    #[test]
    fn bonds_are_symmetric() {
        let m = chain(4);
        for i in 0..4u8 {
            for j in 0..4u8 {
                assert_eq!(m.bond_order(i, j), m.bond_order(j, i));
            }
        }
    }

    #[test]
    fn degree_counts_all_orders() {
        let mut m = Mol12::new();
        for _ in 0..3 {
            m.add_atom(ElementId(0));
        }
        m.add_bond(0, 1, 1);
        m.add_bond(0, 2, 3);
        assert_eq!(m.degree(0), 2);
        assert_eq!(m.degree(1), 1);
    }

    #[test]
    fn mass_is_the_exact_sum_of_atoms() {
        let u = Universe::generate(5);
        let m = chain(6);
        let expected: borbax_units::Mass =
            (0..6).map(|i| u.element(ElementId(i % 4)).mass).sum();
        // Exact equality, not a tolerance — this is why Mass is fixed-point.
        assert_eq!(m.mass(&u), expected);
    }

    #[test]
    fn connectivity_is_detected() {
        assert!(chain(5).is_connected());
        let mut m = chain(4);
        m.add_atom(ElementId(0)); // isolated fifth atom
        assert!(!m.is_connected());
    }

    #[test]
    fn capacity_is_respected() {
        let mut m = Mol12::new();
        for _ in 0..MAX_ATOMS + 3 {
            m.add_atom(ElementId(0));
        }
        assert_eq!(m.n as usize, MAX_ATOMS);
    }
}
```

```rust
//! Small-molecule atom graphs (spec §8.1).
//!
//! Capped at twelve atoms. (An earlier version said "which lets the whole
//! molecule live in 72 bytes" — 72 was the adjacency block alone, never the
//! struct. Measured with `size_of` on 1.97.1: **86 bytes at three planes, 158 at
//! six**. Quote the measurement or drop the number.)
//!
//! Adjacency is stored as bitset rows. That matters more than it might
//! appear: colour refinement in `canonical.rs` becomes a sequence of
//! popcounts over masks, with no allocation and no hashing anywhere in the
//! hot path.

use borbax_units::Mass;
use borbax_universe::{ElementId, Universe};

pub const MAX_ATOMS: usize = 12;
/// One bitset plane per representable bond order.
///
/// **Derive this from `borbax_universe::BondOrder::PLANES`, do not write 3 and
/// do not write `ALL.len()`.**
/// Task 5 measured that the per-universe ceiling of `min(valence_a, valence_b)`
/// is `{4, 5, 6}` and **never 3**, and raised `BondOrder`'s cap accordingly —
/// but nothing told this file, so `Mol12` could represent only half the orders
/// the universe admits and `add_bond` dropped the rest silently. That is the
/// same cross-constant drift Task 5 closed *inside* `borbax-universe`, escaping
/// across the crate boundary, and the migration tables that exist to catch it
/// were themselves stale.
/// `ALL.len()` was the first fix and it is subtly wrong: this array is indexed
/// at `order - 1`, so what it needs is `MAX`, not the *count* of representable
/// orders. They agree only while `ALL` is dense, which is guaranteed by a
/// `const _` inside `borbax-universe` that this crate can neither see nor cite.
/// A sparse `ALL` — `[1, 2, 3, 6]`, not absurd while formability is being
/// decided — makes `ALL.len()` 4 while `MAX` is 6, and order 6 indexes plane 5
/// of a 4-plane array. `BondOrder::PLANES` states what this code needs;
/// `ALL.len()` states an adjacent thing that currently coincides.
pub const N_ORDERS: usize = borbax_universe::BondOrder::PLANES;

/// **Fields are private, and `elem` holds `ElementId` rather than `u8`.**
///
/// Two findings, one shape. `pub adj` lets any external write set two planes for
/// one pair, after which `bond_order` silently returns the *lowest* order and
/// `valence_used` counts both — an illegal state maintained only by `add_bond`
/// remembering to call `clear_bond` first. Task 11's `Polymer` will be the first
/// thing to build a `Mol12` by another route. And `[u8; _]` cannot be written or
/// read at all from here: `ElementId`'s field and its `index()` are both
/// `pub(crate)` in `borbax-universe`, deliberately, so `self.elem[i] = e.0` and
/// `ElementId(self.elem[i])` are hard compile errors outside that crate.
/// `[ElementId; MAX_ATOMS]` is the same byte (asserted by a `const _` there),
/// stays `Copy`, and cannot hold a value that was never an id.
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq)]
pub struct Mol12 {
    n: u8,
    elem: [ElementId; MAX_ATOMS],
    /// `adj[order.plane_index()][i]` is a bitset of atoms bonded to `i` at that
    /// order.
    adj: [[u16; MAX_ATOMS]; N_ORDERS],
}

impl Default for Mol12 {
    fn default() -> Self {
        Self::new()
    }
}

impl Mol12 {
    #[must_use]
    pub const fn new() -> Self {
        Self { n: 0, elem: [ElementId::ZERO; MAX_ATOMS], adj: [[0; MAX_ATOMS]; N_ORDERS] }
    }

    /// Append an atom. Silently ignored at capacity — a molecule that cannot
    /// grow is chemically meaningful (it simply does not react that way),
    /// whereas a panic here would take down a simulation step.
    pub fn add_atom(&mut self, e: ElementId) -> Option<u8> {
        if self.n as usize >= MAX_ATOMS {
            return None;
        }
        let idx = self.n;
        self.elem[idx as usize] = e;
        self.n += 1;
        Some(idx)
    }

    /// Add or replace a bond.
    ///
    /// **Takes a `BondOrder`, and the plane comes from the order.** The
    /// predecessor took `u8` and computed `(order - 1) as usize`. Deleting its
    /// stale `1..=3` guard without changing the signature converted a
    /// silent-drop defect into a **panic**: verified on 1.97.1, `order == 0`
    /// overflows the subtraction in debug and wraps to 255 in release (where
    /// `overflow-checks` is off) then indexes out of bounds, and `order >= 7`
    /// indexes out of bounds in both. `bond_order` returns the no-bond sentinel
    /// `0`, and `permute` feeds its output straight back here behind an
    /// `if o > 0` — drop that guard and canonicalisation aborts a run.
    ///
    /// Making `bond_order` return `Option<BondOrder>` is what makes the whole
    /// class unreachable rather than merely guarded, and it retires a
    /// `0`-means-absent sentinel of exactly the shape `bonds.rs` spends forty
    /// lines condemning in `Quanta::ZERO` — in a type where `0` is
    /// unrepresentable and `Option<BondOrder>` is 2 bytes, measured.
    pub fn add_bond(&mut self, a: u8, b: u8, order: BondOrder) -> Result<(), BondError> {
        // **Not a silent early return.** These are caller bugs by the same
        // argument the predecessor's own comment made about the order, and it
        // then left them silent — while `add_atom` four lines above argues the
        // *opposite* for its out-of-range case. Two adjacent functions, same
        // question, opposite answers. They differ for a real reason and it needs
        // saying: a molecule at capacity is chemically meaningful (it simply does
        // not react that way), whereas an out-of-range atom index is a bug in the
        // caller's bookkeeping with no chemical reading at all.
        if a == b || a >= self.n || b >= self.n {
            return Err(BondError::NoSuchAtom { a, b, n: self.n });
        }
        self.clear_bond(a, b);
        let plane = order.plane_index();
        self.adj[plane][a as usize] |= 1 << b;
        self.adj[plane][b as usize] |= 1 << a;
        Ok(())
    }

    pub fn clear_bond(&mut self, a: u8, b: u8) {
        for plane in &mut self.adj {
            plane[a as usize] &= !(1 << b);
            plane[b as usize] &= !(1 << a);
        }
    }

    /// **`Option<BondOrder>`, not a `0`-means-absent `u8`.** The sentinel form
    /// is what let `permute` feed a `0` back into `add_bond`, and it is the
    /// same shape `bonds.rs` spends forty lines condemning in `Quanta::ZERO` —
    /// in a type where `0` is unrepresentable and `Option<BondOrder>` costs 2
    /// bytes, measured. `permute` becomes `if let Some(o) = ...`.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn bond_order(&self, a: u8, b: u8) -> Option<BondOrder> {
        BondOrder::ALL
            .iter()
            .find(|o| self.adj[o.plane_index()][a as usize] & (1 << b) != 0)
            .copied()
    }

    /// Number of bonded neighbours, regardless of order.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn degree(&self, a: u8) -> u32 {
        self.neighbours(a).count_ones()
    }

    /// Bitset of all neighbours of `a` at any order.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn neighbours(&self, a: u8) -> u16 {
        self.adj.iter().fold(0, |acc, plane| acc | plane[a as usize])
    }

    /// Total valence consumed by bonds, counting order.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn valence_used(&self, a: u8) -> u32 {
        self.adj
            .iter()
            .enumerate()
            .map(|(i, plane)| (i as u32 + 1) * plane[a as usize].count_ones())
            .sum()
    }

    /// Exact total mass. Integer addition, so this is a guarantee rather
    /// than a tolerance (spec §13.1).
    #[must_use]
    pub fn mass(&self, u: &Universe) -> Mass {
        (0..self.n).map(|i| u.element(self.elem[i as usize]).mass).sum()
    }

    #[must_use]
    pub fn is_connected(&self) -> bool {
        if self.n == 0 {
            return true;
        }
        let mut seen: u16 = 1;
        let mut frontier: u16 = 1;
        while frontier != 0 {
            let mut next: u16 = 0;
            for i in 0..self.n {
                if frontier & (1 << i) != 0 {
                    next |= self.neighbours(i);
                }
            }
            next &= !seen;
            seen |= next;
            frontier = next;
        }
        seen.count_ones() == u32::from(self.n)
    }
}
```
